### Notta

<https://www.notta.ai/>

_Added in wave 2, to close a gap the completeness critic identified._

**Positioning**

Not a thought-capture tool at all — an AI MEETING notetaker. Homepage: "an AI meeting notetaker that turns your meetings, interviews, or lectures into searchable text or visuals", with the 2026 emphasis shifted from transcripts to deliverables: "Generate ready-to-be-shared PPT files", "Create structured visuals in 5 seconds", "Get actionable and valuable information across all recordings". The unit of work is a MEETING, not a thought. Its differentiator versus Otter/Fireflies/Fathom is language breadth (58 transcription / 42 translation languages) plus a bot-free desktop recorder and its own $149 hardware. The Japanese App Store name is language-first: "Notta-自動文字起こし･日本語の音声をテキスト変換" (automatic transcription — convert Japanese audio to text), which tells you where the business actually is.

**Maturity and evidence of use**

iOS app original release 2019-12-19 (iTunes lookup id 1480649572, US) — nearly seven years old. Publisher: Mind Cruiser Limited. First-party scale claims (https://www.notta.ai/en/about): "10 million+ users", "6,000+ companies", "30 million+ hours" transcribed — unaudited. No founding story, founder names, HQ or funding are published on the about page (unverified — I could not confirm any of these). Certifications claimed and repeated on https://www.notta.ai/en/security: SOC 2 Type II, ISO/IEC 27001:2013, GDPR, CCPA, HIPAA; TLS 1.2 in transit, AES-256 at rest, hosted on AWS (S3 + Aurora), with a named CPO and CISO. Whether audio is used for model training is NOT addressed on that page, and no retention or deletion policy is stated there (unverified). RATINGS, all fetched 2026-08-31: Japan App Store 4.35 (averageUserRating 4.34588) across 26,405 ratings, version 6.79.14, updated 2026-08-26. US App Store 4.05 across 1,398 ratings. Japan therefore holds ~19x the US rating volume and a full third of a star more — Notta is a Japanese-market product with an English website. Notta is NOT on the Chinese App Store under this name (the "Notta" returned by an itunes.apple.com CN search is trackId 6794847648, seller 嘉奇 龙, an unrelated local-first markdown notes app with 0 ratings). Latest US release note adds "Portuguese (Brazil) Support". The public changelog (https://www.notta.ai/en/changelog) is badly stale — the newest dated entry I could retrieve is January 31st, 2025, with no 2026 entries, despite the app shipping v6.79.14 in August 2026. INDEPENDENT SIGNAL, and it is bad: Trustpilot (https://www.trustpilot.com/review/notta.ai) shows the company's TrustScore REMOVED for a breach of Trustpilot guidelines with fake reviews identified and removed; 172 reviews total, 26 in the last 12 months, distribution 85% 1-star, 6% 5-star, most recent reviews dated 2026-08-31. Recurring Trustpilot themes: unauthorised charges after free trials, transcripts stopping mid-process, poor speaker separation, refused refunds. G2 (https://www.g2.com/products/notta/reviews) blocked fetching with 403, so I have no G2 figures (unverified). Weigh the Trustpilot picture against 26,405 Japanese ratings at 4.35 — the app works, the billing does not.

**Platforms**

iOS · Android · Web app (app.notta.ai) · macOS desktop (bot-free recorder + Privacy Mode beta) · Windows desktop (bot-free recorder + Privacy Mode beta) · Chrome extension · Notta Memo hardware recorder ($149) · Zenchord hardware (paired device)

**Pricing**

From https://www.notta.ai/en/pricing (fetched 2026-08-31). Free: $0, "120 transcription minutes/month", "Up to 3 minutes per conversation", "50 file uploads/month", "10 AI Summary/month". Pro: "$8.17/month" billed annually at "$97.99/year" (stated 40% off), "1,800 transcription minutes/month", "Up to 5 hours per recording", "100 file uploads/month", "100 AI Summary/month". Business: "$16.67/month" billed annually at "$199.99/year", "Unlimited transcription", 5h per recording, "200 file uploads/month", "200 AI Summary/month", customisable seats. Enterprise: custom, from 51 seats, SAML SSO, full data access control. The 3-minute-per-conversation cap on Free is the crucial number: the free tier is a demo, not a usable product. Notta Brain (cross-meeting Q&A, infographics, slides) runs on a SEPARATE "AI credits" pool — "Your monthly credit allowance depends on your Notta Brain plan", and standard transcription plans "don't automatically include additional Notta Brain credits" (https://www.notta.ai/en/features/notta-brain). Hardware: Notta Memo at "$149.00 USD". Japanese reviewers complain about exactly this stacking — one 1-star JP review cites an additional ¥30,000 for unlimited translation (iTunes JP reviews RSS, id 1480649572, v6.76.16).

**Capture UX**

Five capture routes (https://www.notta.ai/en): online meeting recorder (bot joins Zoom/Meet/Teams/Webex), BOT-FREE desktop recorder (records system audio locally, no visible bot in the call), file/link upload, browser screen recorder, and the Notta Memo hardware. Mobile (https://www.notta.ai/en/mobile): record audio with simultaneous live transcript, import audio/video files, add bookmarks mid-recording "for important moments during a recording as a reminder", 3x playback, keyword search, auto-sync across platforms. NO widget, NO lock-screen capture, NO Siri Shortcut, NO Apple Watch documented — capture is a deliberate app-open action because a meeting is a scheduled event, not an impulse. Length limits are the plan caps: 3 minutes per conversation on Free, up to 5 hours per recording on paid. Notta Memo (https://www.notta.ai/en/hardware/memo): 28 grams, "3.39" × 2.17" × 0.14" (86.1 × 55.1 × 3.5 mm)", "4 MEMS microphones + 1 bone conduction microphone", ~10 ft / 3 m pickup, "470mAh" battery giving "30 hours of continuous recording / 28 days of standby", "32GB" storage, 1.5h magnetic charge, records fully OFFLINE then syncs over "Bluetooth & Wi-Fi" and is "automatically transcribed into searchable notes". Two modes: Call Recording (triggered by phone vibration detection) and Live Recording. The hardware is the only genuinely one-gesture capture Notta has, and it costs $149.

**Processing and AI**

Claimed "98.86% transcription accuracy" (App Store description) / "98%+ Accuracy Guaranteed" (homepage) — vendor claim, unverified independently. "58 Languages Supported" for transcription and 42 for translation (homepage, App Store description, and the Dutch page: "Notta ondersteunt maar liefst 58 verschillende talen voor transcriptie en vertaling"). No engine or model is named anywhere I fetched. LANGUAGE DETAIL THAT MATTERS: the full 58-language list is NOT published on any page I could reach (the help centre at https://support.notta.ai/hc/en-us returns 403 to fetchers), so per-language quality is unverifiable. What IS verifiable is the on-device fallback set, which is the honest signal of which languages they actually invest in — Privacy Mode (https://www.notta.ai/en/features/privacy-mode, Mac + Windows desktop, Beta, included with Pro, "does not consume cloud transcription minutes"): the LOCAL model supports auto-detect, Simplified Chinese, English, Japanese, Korean and Cantonese ONLY; routing to Apple Speech Analyzer adds German, French, Spanish, Italian, Portuguese and Traditional Chinese. DUTCH IS IN NEITHER LIST. Dutch is therefore cloud-only, inside the unpublished 58, and cannot be verified for quality; treat "Dutch supported" as claimed-but-unverified. Explicit accuracy tradeoff stated: "Use Privacy Mode when privacy matters more than maximum accuracy". CODE-SWITCHING: Notta does NOT do free-form code-switching. Bilingual transcription (https://www.notta.ai/en/features/bilingual-transcription) requires you to "Select two official languages under the 'Bilingual transcription' tab" in advance, and covers only "10+ languages" — a much smaller set than 58. The page's FAQ claims tools like Notta "support multiple languages and can accurately handle code-switching" but gives no mechanism; the product reality is a pre-declared language PAIR, not detection. Speaker diarisation with editable speaker names. Custom dictionary: 3 terms free, up to 200 on Premium. AI layer: AI Notes generates "summaries & action items", "key points", "decisions made", from default or user-authored prompt templates ("craft your own prompts and develop custom templates", saved into "a curated library"). Notta Brain is a chat over the whole workspace — "Ask questions across all your meetings", @-mention "any meeting, PDF, Docs, or Excel file", and generate infographics, slide decks, reports, follow-up email drafts, executive summaries. Throughput claim: "Import video or audio and get a one-hour recording transcribed in about five minutes".

**Data model**

A meeting-recording model, not a note model.

RECORDING / TRANSCRIPT (the root entity): audio or video, plus a timestamped transcript. Attributes: title, date, duration, source (live mic / meeting bot / desktop bot-free / upload / Memo device), language(s), and the media file itself (audio IS retained — playback with 3x speed and silence-skip is a documented feature).

SEGMENT / LINE: each transcript line carries a timestamp and a SPEAKER label. "Notta's AI identifies different speakers in the conversation. You can edit the speakers' names" (https://www.notta.ai/en/web). Speakers are labels on segments, NOT a first-class people entity with roles — there is no person record that links across meetings, no contact directory, nothing like our people M2M.

BOOKMARK / HIGHLIGHT — this is the closest thing Notta has to our action_type, and it is worth copying: a bookmark on a transcript segment is TYPED. "Bookmark important text as Key point, To-do or Project in the transcripts, and the progress bar will automatically show highlights in the corresponding moments." So a three-value enum {Key point, To-do, Project} applied to a span of a transcript, rendered as markers on the audio scrubber. Notably these are set by the HUMAN during or after recording, not proposed by AI, and there is no evidence they flow into any task system.

AI NOTE: a generated artefact attached to a recording, produced from a TEMPLATE (default or user-authored prompt). Fields are template-defined but documented ones are summary, key points, decisions made, action items assigned. Editable. Not a separate queryable entity — it hangs off the recording.

FOLDER: folders exist. The changelog entry for January 31st, 2025 (https://www.notta.ai/en/changelog) reads "Automatic transcription enabled for files moved to Agent folder" — i.e. folders are containers AND can carry automation rules. Beyond that, folder semantics are undocumented on public pages (the help centre 403s). There is a WORKSPACE concept implied by Notta Brain searching "all meetings" in your workspace and by Business/Enterprise seat management.

NO TAGS documented. NO project entity (Project is only a bookmark LABEL). NO person entity. NO status field, no due date, no reminder. NO INBOX AND NO UNPROCESSED STATE AT ALL — and it doesn't need one, because a recording arrives already knowing what it is (a meeting), when it happened, and who spoke. Context comes from the calendar and the microphone, not from an AI guess. That is the structural reason Notta has no confidence gate: it never has to infer intent.

CAPTURE→TASK/PROJECT/PERSON: essentially absent. The AI extracts "action items assigned" as TEXT inside a generated note. Nothing on the marketing pages says action items become trackable objects or sync to a task manager. The escape hatch is export/integration: push the recap to Slack, email or Notion and let the destination be the task system. Salesforce and HubSpot integrations imply meeting-to-CRM-record mapping, which is the one real capture→entity link they have, and it is enterprise-shaped (contact/opportunity), not personal.

**Organisation and retrieval**

Retrieval is keyword search within a transcript plus timestamps ("Use timestamps and Search option to locate keywords quickly"), folders, and — the 2026 answer — Notta Brain as semantic chat across the whole workspace: "spots the patterns, tracks the changes, and surfaces what matters most across weeks of discussions", with @-mention referencing of specific meetings and attached PDFs/Docs/Excel. Notta's answer to organisation is therefore: don't organise, ask the LLM. That is the same bet as flomo's, arrived at from the opposite direction — flomo resurfaces without asking, Notta answers when asked. Neither requires the user to file. The progress-bar highlight markers give a visual retrieval layer inside a single long recording that most transcript tools lack.

**Outputs and integrations**

Export: "TXT, DOCX, Excel, PDF, or SRT", with or without timestamps, plus secure share links; "Customize options to include key elements in the exported file". Generated deliverables: PPT/slide decks, infographics, reports, follow-up email drafts, executive summaries. Integrations (from the site nav): Zoom, Google Meet, Microsoft Teams, Webex, Slack, Notion, Salesforce, HubSpot, Google Calendar, Outlook Calendar, Google Drive, Zapier. Recap distribution "via email, Slack message, or Notion export". Hardware pairing with Notta Memo and Zenchord. Platforms: Web app, iOS, Android, Mac, Windows, Chrome extension.

**Strengths**

- Language breadth as the product, not a footnote: 58 transcription / 42 translation languages is the reason it beats Otter and Fireflies outside English, and it is what built the Japanese base.
- On-device Privacy Mode on desktop that does NOT consume cloud minutes — a genuine differentiator for regulated buyers and a clean answer to 'where does my audio go'.
- Bot-free desktop recording: no bot joins the call, no consent-theatre, works with any meeting platform including ones with no integration.
- Typed transcript bookmarks {Key point, To-do, Project} rendered as markers on the audio progress bar — cheap, human-driven structure inside a long recording.
- User-authored AI templates saved to a reusable library, so the output shape is the user's, not the vendor's.
- Notta Brain: semantic chat across the entire corpus with @-mention of specific meetings and attached documents — retrieval without filing.
- Deliverable-shaped output (PPT, infographics, follow-up email drafts), which is what a meeting buyer actually needs, not another transcript.
- Real hardware with 30h recording / 28-day standby / 32GB and offline capture that auto-syncs — the only true zero-friction path in their stack.
- Enterprise-grade compliance posture (SOC 2 Type II, ISO 27001, GDPR, CCPA, HIPAA claims) that a solo-built app cannot match, plus SAML SSO at Enterprise.
- Deep meeting-stack integrations: Zoom, Meet, Teams, Webex, Slack, Notion, Salesforce, HubSpot, both calendars, Drive, Zapier.

**Weaknesses**

- Billing reputation is the standing wound: Trustpilot rating REMOVED for guideline breach with fake reviews identified, 85% of 172 reviews at 1 star, dominated by charges after free trials and refused refunds. A Japanese 1-star review cites ¥30,000 extra for unlimited translation.
- The 58-language list is not published anywhere fetchable and the help centre 403s, so per-language quality — including Dutch — is unverifiable. The on-device language set (zh-Hans, EN, JA, KO, Cantonese, plus Apple's DE/FR/ES/IT/PT/zh-Hant) reveals where the real investment is, and Dutch is not in it.
- No real code-switching. 'Bilingual transcription' means pre-declaring TWO languages from a '10+ languages' subset. A Flemish speaker dropping English terms mid-sentence — the normal case in Belgium — is not the supported path.
- Free tier is 3 minutes per conversation. That is a demo, and it is why trial-to-charge friction generates so many complaints.
- Notta Brain runs on a separate AI-credit pool that transcription plans don't include — a second paywall behind the first, and the source of the 'hidden cost' complaints.
- Public changelog is stale by 19 months (newest dated entry January 31st, 2025) while the app ships weekly — poor transparency for a tool holding your recordings.
- No published data-retention or deletion policy, and no statement on whether audio is used for model training, on the security page.
- No thought-capture surface at all: no widget, no lock-screen entry, no Shortcut, no Watch. It cannot catch an idea in a car park.
- No people, project, task, tag or status entities. 'Action items' are text inside a generated note; nothing tracks them.
- Privacy Mode is Mac/Windows desktop Beta only — not on mobile, which is where most recording happens.
- US traction is thin (1,398 ratings at 4.05) against Japan's 26,405 at 4.35 — the English-market position is weak despite an English-first website.
- Absent from the Chinese App Store, so the 'Chinese market' influence is linguistic (zh-Hans, Cantonese support) rather than distributional.

**Ideas for Delta**

- The most transferable thing here is the typed bookmark: {Key point, To-do, Project} applied to a SPAN of a transcript, shown as markers on the audio scrubber. Our action_type currently applies to a whole capture. Letting one capture yield several typed spans is closer to how people actually ramble, and the scrubber markers make the AI's reading auditable at a glance.
- Steal 'no inbox because context is known'. Notta needs no triage because the calendar and the microphone tell it what a recording is. Where WE can borrow that: time of day, location, whether the phone was in a car, and the last-used project are cheap priors that could push a capture over the 0.75 gate without a better model.
- Publish the language list. Notta's biggest verifiable weakness is that nobody can check which of its 58 languages are good. For a Dutch/Flemish-first product, a published, honest per-language quality statement — including where we are weak — is a positioning asset our larger competitors structurally cannot copy.
- Code-switching is a real, unserved gap in Belgium. Notta requires you to pre-declare a language PAIR from a '10+' subset. Handling NL↔EN mid-sentence without configuration would be a defensible wedge, and it is squarely in the market we are already targeting.
- Copy user-authored AI templates: let the user write the prompt that shapes the 'understand' output and save it. It converts our fixed intent taxonomy into something users can bend without us shipping code.
- Copy the two-tier billing structure BUT NOT the execution. Separating transcription minutes from AI credits is sound unit economics; hiding it until after the charge is what generated 85% 1-star reviews. Show both meters in the app before the trial converts.
- On-device transcription that does not consume cloud quota is the single strongest trust feature they have, and Whisper-class local models on modern phones make it reachable for us. Frame it as 'your audio never leaves the phone unless you ask'.
- A daily/weekly semantic Q&A over all captures ('what did I say about X this month') is now table stakes at the top of this category — Notta Brain and flomo Agent both ship it. If we don't have it, we look 2024.
- Their free tier caps LENGTH (3 min/conversation), not count. For a zero-typing-friction pitch, capping length is the worst possible choice — it punishes the rambling thought we exist to catch. Cap count or AI operations instead.
- The Notta Memo's 470mAh / 30h / 32GB offline-then-sync design is the physical form of what our app should feel like: record blind, sync later, never block on connectivity. Our offline capture path should be a first-class requirement, not a fallback.
- Their action items go nowhere. That is precisely the seam our actions table is built for. 'The recap tells you what to do; we actually keep track' is a real differentiator against the whole meeting-notetaker category.

### Day One

<https://dayoneapp.com/>

_Added in wave 2, to close a gap the completeness critic identified._

**Positioning**

"Your journal for life" / "The #1 journaling app" (dayoneapp.com homepage). A memory vault, not a productivity tool: the promise is that you write, it is preserved and encrypted forever, and it comes back to you later. Marketing leans on trophies (Apple App of the Year, Apple Design Award, Editors' Choice) and on "You own the data, we keep it safe". Note the App Store category is Health & Fitness, not Productivity — Day One sells wellbeing, not throughput. Zero of the marketing language is about doing anything with a capture; it is about reliving it ("Use On This Day to take a trip back in time. No time machine needed").

**Maturity and evidence of use**

Founded March 2011 by Paul Mayne; acquired by Automattic on 14 June 2021, terms undisclosed, with Mayne staying on as CEO (TechCrunch, 14 Jun 2021). TechCrunch cites "over 15 million downloads since launch", Sensor Tower's 4.4 million mobile installs and 993,000 installs in 2020; Day One's own blog says "downloaded over 15 million times in 225 different countries/territories" and describes a "small software development team" retained post-acquisition. US App Store (apps.apple.com/us/app/day-one-journal/id1044867788, checked 31 Aug 2026): 4.8 stars from 118,000 ratings, seller Bloom Built Inc, version 2026.18 shipped the same day I checked, 345.9 MB, iOS 18.0+, 4+, Health & Fitness, 26 languages including Dutch. The homepage claims "over 150,000 five-star reviews" (that is a cross-store aggregate, not the US iOS 118K figure). Release cadence is high — a calendar-versioned build (2026.18) landing within hours, with notes covering Daily Chat Bio, moving read-only entries between journals, a search-index fix and a non-Gregorian-calendar date bug. So: fifteen years old, deep-pocketed corporate parent, small team, still shipping weekly. Google Play figures unverified — I could not resolve a working Play listing URL.

**Platforms**

iPhone · iPad · Apple Watch (watchOS) · Mac · Android · Windows · Web · Browser extensions

**Pricing**

Three tiers, verified on https://dayoneapp.com/pricing/ (Aug 2026). Basic $0: unlimited text entries, unlimited journals, 1 photo per entry, end-to-end encryption, daily prompts, templates, search & filters, memories, streaks. Silver $49.99/year: 30 photos/videos per entry, unlimited device sync, audio recordings & transcription, Strava/Zapier/IFTTT, PDF scanning, drawings, custom colours/icons, 25% off printed journals. Gold $74.99/year: adds Daily Chat, AI summaries & prompts, entry summaries, title suggestions, image generation, 35% off printed books. The US App Store IAP list (apps.apple.com/us/app/day-one-journal/id1044867788) shows Day One Silver monthly $8.99, Silver annual $49.99, Gold annual $74.99, plus legacy "Premium Yearly $49.99". The pricing/features guide adds the two limits that matter: Basic syncs to 1 active device only, and Silver/Gold allow "up to 30 media per entry (photos, videos, audio, PDF/scans)" with a 5-minute video cap. Free-month trial exists but "free trial eligibility is controlled by the App Stores". Historic anchor: an Android Police review dated 19 Jan 2025 quotes Premium at "$6/month or $35/year" — so the annual price rose roughly 43% to $49.99 and a second, higher AI tier was added on top within about eighteen months.

**Capture UX**

Multiple ingest channels, all of them ending in the same place — an entry in a journal. In-app: + icon from List, Calendar, Media or Map view; long-press the app icon for a direct new entry. Audio: tap the audio icon (iOS) or paperclip > audio (Android); a long-press on the microphone icon on iOS starts transcription mode instead of plain recording. Apple Watch: record audio up to 3 hours, and it works "while not connected to iPhone", syncing when reconnected — genuinely offline capture from the wrist; you can also dictate, sketch, drop emoji, or just "Check In" from your current location; there is an Infograph complication. Share extension and browser extensions. Email-to-entry. SMS-to-entry: register your number, then text Day One; "#hashtags in your message" become tags automatically and every texted entry gets a "From Text" tag — but it is US-only via SMS and Canada-only via WhatsApp, quota'd at 500 messages/year (annual plans) or 50/month (monthly), with top-ups at $0.99 for 50 and $4.99 for 300, and location is NOT captured via text. Recording length: standard mode up to 3 hours with no transcription; transcription mode capped at 10 minutes. Up to 30 recordings in a single entry, .m4a. macOS, Windows and Web cannot record at all — import only. There is no locked-device one-tap capture equivalent to a hardware button; the Watch app is the closest thing.

**Processing and AI**

Transcription is Apple's, not Day One's. The guide states transcription is optional and must be enabled in settings — it is NOT automatic — and it "prioritizes the keyboard language" falling back to the device language. On iOS 26 with Apple Intelligence "transcription runs entirely on-device"; on older versions it goes to "Apple's servers". Transcription is iOS-only: Android records but cannot transcribe, and the Apple Watch cannot transcribe either. Dutch is therefore transcribable in Day One only because Apple's on-device Speech stack supports it — Day One itself owns none of that pipeline. Separately there is an AI layer, Gold-only (v2026.7+), with a daily usage cap: Daily Chat (conversational journaling that ends in "Generate Entry", with a persistent "Bio" memory of work, schools, places, people and pets per the 2026.18 release notes), Go Deeper prompts, Multi-Entry Summary, Entry Highlights, Title Suggestions, Image Generation. Provider is deliberately vague: "The AI model services used may be hosted by our own company, Automattic, or may be a third-party service like OpenAI"; Daily Chat Voice Mode explicitly uses "OpenAI's Realtime API". Crucially none of this AI extracts intent, tasks, people or projects — it summarises, titles, prompts and illustrates. There is no classification step, no confidence gate, no routing. The app is localised into 26 languages including Dutch, but the AI features' language coverage is undocumented (unverified).

**Data model**

Flat-ish, two-level, with rich per-entry metadata and no action layer. ENTITIES: (1) Journal — the container. Attributes: name, colour, icon, cover image, description, default template, plus three behaviour flags that matter: "Include in Streaks", include-in-On-This-Day/Calendar, and its encryption mode (Standard vs E2EE). Journals can be merged and deleted; entries can be moved between them. (2) Collection — a named folder of journals, added recently, explicitly single-level ("Collections cannot be nested inside other collections"), Silver/Gold only. So the hierarchy is Collection > Journal > Entry and stops there. (3) Entry — belongs to exactly one journal. Body is Markdown-capable rich text with headings, bold/italic, blockquotes, checklists and tables. Auto/attached metadata surfaced through filters: date, location (editable, plus saved/named locations), weather, activity, music, creation device, favourite flag, checklist presence, published state. (4) Tag — flat, no nesting documented, no limits documented. Tags live in a global pool but the picker lets you "filter the tag list to show tags from All Journals or just the Current Journal", so a tag is effectively global with journal-scoped views. Tags can be renamed and deleted globally, and applied to many entries at once. (5) Media — photos, videos (5-min cap), audio (.m4a, ≤30 per entry), PDFs/scans, drawings. Attachments hang off entries; audio carries an optional transcript. (6) Template — static text only: "Day One templates are static – they store fixed text, formatting, checklists, and prompts. There is no variable substitution syntax in templates." No structured fields, no {{date}}. (7) Prompt — daily rotating writing prompt plus purchasable/curated Prompt Packs. (8) Shared Journal — up to 30 members including the owner, invite links expiring after 14 days, members can add entries and comment/react, "entries in a shared journal can only be edited by the shared journal member who created the entry", ownership transferable, and free for all tiers. LINKS: entry-to-entry links exist but are manual Markdown links generated via "Copy Markdown Link"; a link picker (v2026.13+) is iOS/macOS only. There are NO backlinks — the destination entry does not know it was referenced. WHAT DOES NOT EXIST: no task/action entity, no status field, no due dates, no people entity (contacts are not modelled; a person is just a word in text or a tag), no project entity, and critically NO INBOX OR UNPROCESSED STATE. Every capture lands directly in a journal, final, filed by the act of choosing a journal at write time. There is no triage queue, no "needs review" flag, nothing between capture and archive. The only bulk-processing affordances are multi-select move-to-journal and multi-select tag (plus, on iOS, Edit Location, Export, Merge, Delete; web caps selection at 100 entries) — which is a batch cleanup tool, not a workflow. A capture never becomes a task, and there is no mechanism by which it could.

**Organisation and retrieval**

Four retrieval surfaces, three of them time-shaped. (1) SEARCH: there is a real local full-text index — a rebuildable FTS database, at ~/Library/Group Containers/5U8NS4GX82.dayoneapp2/Data/Documents/indexes/fts.db on macOS. The 2026.18 release notes list "Fixed problems rebuilding the search index", so it is actively fragile. E2EE journals are not indexed by Spotlight ("Day One stores entries in an encrypted database inside its app container, and encrypted journals are not indexed by Spotlight"), so search is in-app only. No documented search operators. v2026.11 improved CJK substring matching. Which fields are indexed (body, titles, tags, audio transcripts, photo OCR) is not documented — unverified. (2) FILTERS, richest on macOS/Web/Windows: "Photo, Audio, Video, PDF, Place, Favorite, Tags, Untagged, Date (specific years only), Checklist, Published, Activity, Music, On This Day". iOS adds an Any/All toggle when several tags are picked. Android is the poor relation — Favourites, Tags, Places, keywords, and no date filtering at all. Note "Date (specific years only)" — you cannot filter to an arbitrary range on macOS. (3) TIME VIEWS: Calendar view, Today View (all platforms), List view with customisable density, Media gallery, Map view. (4) RESURFACING, the real product: On This Day shows entries from the same calendar date in prior years and can push a daily notification, with per-journal opt-out — but it is iOS/macOS/Android only, "not available on Web or Windows apps yet". Today View embeds an On This Day section at the bottom. Streaks count consecutive days entries were created, appear in the More menu and per-journal stats, offer a calendar of missed days for "restoration", have a per-journal "Include in Streaks" toggle, and — telling — "At this time, there is no way to fully disable the Streaks feature". Daily writing prompts rotate every day, on by default for English-language devices. Net: a Day One user finds things by remembering roughly when, or by being handed something on an anniversary. Tags and filters are a secondary, power-user path.

**Outputs and integrations**

Export: JSON (a zip with a .json of all selected entries plus optional photos/videos/audios/pdfs folders — the re-import format), PDF (page sizes Letter/A3/A4, configurable fonts and which metadata to print: date, time, location, tags, weather, plus optional monthly summary pages with maps), plain text/Markdown ("the text preserves markdown syntax ... **bold**, _italic_, # heading, > quote", photos not included), and CSV on iOS/Android only which "currently contains entry text and the date" — nothing else. Integrations, Silver+: Zapier, IFTTT (works with encrypted journals, may need reconnection), Strava, email-to-entry, SMS/WhatsApp-to-entry, browser extensions, a documented URL scheme, and slash commands in the editor. Instagram integration has been discontinued. The genuinely differentiated output is physical: printed Day One Books, with tier-based discounts (25% Silver, 35% Gold) — an output nobody else in this category monetises. Publishing to WordPress.com/Tumblr was the stated Automattic rationale in 2021; a "Published" filter state exists, which implies it shipped. Notably there is no outbound task/calendar integration at all: nothing goes to Reminders, Todoist, Things or a calendar, because there is nothing task-shaped to send.

**Strengths**

- Fifteen years of continuous shipping under an owner with money. The switching cost of a decade of entries is the moat; nothing about the feature set is.

**Weaknesses**

- No inbox, no triage, no status. A capture is filed the instant it is made by picking a journal, and there is nothing between raw thought and permanent archive. That is a feature for a diary and a disqualifier for anything action-shaped.

**Ideas for Delta**

- The per-journal behaviour flags are the pattern worth stealing wholesale: "Include in Streaks", include-in-On-This-Day/Calendar, per-journal encryption mode. Our Projects should carry the same kind of per-container switches (include in briefs, include in resurfacing, notify on new items) so one setting governs a whole class of captures instead of us adding a per-capture toggle.

### Apple Journal

<https://apps.apple.com/us/app/journal/id6447391597>

_Added in wave 2, to close a gap the completeness critic identified._

**Positioning**

"Reflect on life's moments" (App Store description). Apple's framing is wellbeing and habit, not record-keeping: capture experiences via photos, videos, audio, locations and mood, get intelligent Journaling Suggestions from your own device activity, build a streak. It is a first-party wellness surface that happens to store text, sitting alongside Health's State of Mind. It makes no claim to be a notebook, a second brain, or a place you go looking for things.

**Maturity and evidence of use**

Apple Inc., free, no IAP. US App Store listing (apps.apple.com/us/app/journal/id6447391597, checked 31 Aug 2026): 4.8 stars from 315K ratings, iOS 17.2 or later, 4+; the listing names English as its language, though the app itself is fully localised — Apple's Dutch iPhone User Guide documents it throughout as "Dagboek". Version number, build size and What's New were not exposed on the fetched listing (unverified). Shipped December 2023 with iOS 17.2 as an iPhone-only app; expanded to iPad and Mac with iPadOS 26 / macOS Tahoe, adding Map View, multiple journals and Apple Pencil support (9to5Mac, 5 Jan 2026). Privacy: Apple collects device identifiers and product-interaction analytics not linked to identity. The security posture is the headline number here — per Apple's iCloud data security overview (support.apple.com/en-us/102651), Journal data is end-to-end encrypted with keys on trusted devices under BOTH standard and Advanced Data Protection, putting it in the same always-E2EE class as passwords and Health, whereas Notes, Photos and Voice Memos are only "in transit & on server" with Apple holding the keys unless ADP is switched on. Journal entries are excluded from iCloud backups; capturing them in a backup requires an encrypted local backup. The app can be locked with Face ID, Touch ID or passcode. Independent reads: 9to5Mac (5 Jan 2026) is unreservedly positive about the iPad version with no criticisms offered; Pocket-lint (8 Aug 2025) loves Pencil journaling on iPadOS 26 but reports severe beta lag and device heat on an M1 iPad Air; Tech Journeyman (30 Sep 2025) is the dissent — no markdown, fixed fonts, no nested folders, no Watch app, and concludes Journal "pales in comparison to other free offerings".

**Platforms**

iPhone (iOS 17.2+) · iPad (iPadOS 26+) · Mac (macOS Tahoe+)

**Pricing**

Free. No in-app purchases listed on the US App Store listing. Preinstalled on iPhone from iOS 17.2, and now on iPad and Mac from iPadOS 26 / macOS Tahoe. Storage rides on the user's existing iCloud plan; there is no Journal-specific paywall, no tier, no upsell anywhere in the product.

**Capture UX**

Fast and system-integrated, but always ending in a written entry. In-app: pick a journal, tap +, then either start writing or pick a Journaling Suggestion or a reflection prompt. Home Screen long-press on the Journal icon gives four quick actions — New Entry, View Suggestions, Add Photos, Record Audio — so audio capture is two taps from the Home Screen (though the device must be unlocked; there is no lock-screen or Action-button path documented). Share sheet: from Music, News or any app, tap Share > Journal, write a line, save, and refine later. Widget on Home Screen or Lock Screen showing your current streak or rotating writing prompts, tappable to start an entry. Audio capture happens inside the entry composer: tap the attachment control, tap record, speak, stop. Recording length limits are not documented (unverified). Suggestions are the distinctive capture path — the system pre-assembles outings, photos and workouts into a proposed entry; you can browse the attachments, deselect ones you don't want, then "Add to Entry", or dismiss a suggestion by long-pressing and tapping Delete. Notably there is no wearable: no Apple Watch app for Journal, so unlike Day One there is no wrist capture at all. Offline behaviour is undocumented (unverified), though as a local-first iCloud-synced app it presumably works offline.

**Processing and AI**

Audio is recorded and transcribed inside the entry, but the transcript is NOT auto-inserted — Apple's guide has you tap the recording, tap to view the transcript, then explicitly choose "Add transcript to entry". So the audio remains the primary artefact and the text is an opt-in derivative. The killer constraint, stated flatly in Apple's own iPhone User Guide: "Audiotranscriptie is alleen beschikbaar in het Engels (met uitzondering van de varianten voor Singapore en India)" — audio transcription is English only, excluding the Singapore and India English variants. There is no Dutch transcription in Apple Journal, full stop, even though the app's UI is fully localised into Dutch as "Dagboek". Beyond transcription there is no AI processing of substance: no classification, no intent detection, no entity extraction, no auto-filing. The "intelligence" is entirely in Journaling Suggestions (on-device correlation of workouts, photos, outings, music, contacts and State of Mind) and in Smart notification scheduling, which picks reminder moments "based on your routine and location". Apple Intelligence can summarise entries on supported devices (a user review cites it approvingly) — unverified as to scope and languages. There is also automatic spellcheck and handwriting cleanup for Apple Pencil input on iPad.

**Data model**

Deliberately minimal, two levels, no classification layer. ENTITIES: (1) Journal — since iOS 26 you can create multiple journals; each has a name, a colour and a symbol, and they can be reordered by drag. There is also an "All Entries" pseudo-journal spanning everything. No folders, no nesting, no collections. (2) Entry — belongs to a journal, holds rich formatted text (bold, italic, underline, strikethrough, bulleted and numbered lists, block quotes, text colour) plus attachments. Two distinct dates per entry, which is the single most interesting modelling choice in the app: sorting offers "Datum van notitie" (entry date) and "Datum van moment" (moment date), so an entry written today about last Saturday is filed under both, and the timeline can be read either way. Entries carry a bookmark flag (swipe right to set, bulk-settable via Select Items, and filterable as "Bookmarked"). (3) Attachment — photos, drawings, videos, locations, audio recordings, and State of Mind tiles. Attachments are first-class and spatial: they live in a grid at the top of the entry or can be dragged inline between paragraphs, resized by a corner handle, reordered by drag, viewed full-screen, and removed individually. An audio attachment optionally carries a transcript. Adding a location attachment has a side effect on the model — "Wanneer je een locatie toevoegt, wordt de notitie zichtbaar in 'Plaatsen'", i.e. location membership is what puts an entry on the map. (4) Journaling Suggestion — a system-generated, ephemeral proposal grouping recent outings, photos and workouts. It is not a stored user record: it can be dismissed, it expires, and it only becomes durable when you convert it into an entry. (5) Insights — derived stats, including the current streak ("het aantal opeenvolgende dagen of weken" — days OR weeks), rendered as tappable tiles. WHAT DOES NOT EXIST: no tags whatsoever, no task or action entity, no status, no due date, no project, no people entity (contact references come in only as suggestion attachments), no links between entries, no backlinks, no templates, no custom fields. AND NO INBOX. The closest thing to an unprocessed state is the Journaling Suggestions tray, and it is architecturally the opposite of an inbox: it is the system proposing things you have not captured, not a queue of things you have. Once you write an entry it is filed and finished; the only post-hoc organisation available in the entire app is moving it between journals and setting a bookmark. A capture therefore cannot become a task, cannot be linked to a person, and cannot be assigned to a project — those concepts do not exist in the schema. Export exists though: Day One's importer documents that an Apple Journal export ZIP carries text, titles, dates, photos, videos, audio, maps and locations, music and podcasts, workouts, mood logging and contact references — which is a useful confirmation of the real underlying field set.

**Organisation and retrieval**

Thin, and time-shaped almost exclusively. Pick a journal or "All Entries", then: free-text search across entries; filter by category (Apple's Dutch guide gives "Foto's" and "Reflecties" as examples, so filters are by attachment/entry kind, not by topic); sort by entry date or moment date; jump to a date via the Insights calendar; browse Places, a map view where "Notities met een locatie of foto's met locatie-informatie worden aan de kaart toegevoegd"; and filter to bookmarked entries only. That is the entire retrieval surface. There are no tags, no saved searches, no operators, no cross-entry links, and — importantly for the question — no "On This Day" resurfacing at all. Day One's central reflection mechanic simply does not exist here. Apple's resurfacing is forward-looking instead of backward-looking: Journaling Suggestions push you toward writing about the recent past, Smart notifications nudge by routine and location, the widget rotates prompts, and Insights tiles plus a streak count reward frequency. So Apple has built the habit loop without the memory loop, and it works well enough for 315,000 people to rate it 4.8. Practical consequence: a Journal archive is browsable but not really queryable — you find things by scrolling to roughly the right time, or by remembering they had a photo or a place.

**Outputs and integrations**

Print and export entries (Apple's guide has a dedicated page for it), and the export is structured enough that Day One ships a first-class importer for it. Inbound: the Journaling Suggestions system pulls from Photos, Fitness/workouts, Music, contacts, location and Health's State of Mind, and the same suggestions API is available to third-party developers — so Apple's real integration play is supplying structure to other apps rather than consuming it. Share sheet inbound from any app. iCloud sync across iPhone, iPad and Mac. There is no Zapier/IFTTT/webhook/URL-scheme story, no publishing, no outbound automation, and no task or calendar handoff. Journal is a terminus, by design.

**Strengths**

- Free, preinstalled on every iPhone, and end-to-end encrypted by default with no configuration and no lost-key ceremony — it beats Day One's $49.99/yr E2EE on price, on friction, and on default-safety simultaneously.

**Weaknesses**

- Audio transcription is English-only, explicitly excluding even Singapore and India English variants — so in Dutch/Flemish, Apple Journal's voice capture produces an untranscribed, unsearchable audio blob.

**Ideas for Delta**

- Steal the two-date model immediately: Apple separates entry date (when you recorded it) from moment date (when the thing happened) and lets you sort by either. A voice capture app has exactly this problem — "remind me about the thing from Tuesday's meeting" recorded on Thursday — and our captures/actions tables should carry both occurred_at and captured_at from day one rather than retrofitting it.

<details>
<summary>Sources read (12)</summary>

### Superlist

<https://www.superlist.com/>

_Added in wave 2, to close a gap the completeness critic identified._

**Positioning**

Wunderlist's founding team's second act, explicitly converging on our exact combination. The App Store description opens: "From the team that created Wunderlist: Superlist is the to-do list, notes, and AI meeting app that keeps your work and life organized — and turns every meeting into action items automatically, so nothing slips through the cracks." (itunes lookup id=1547585270). The marketing site leads with "Tasks, notes, and plans. Finally in one app." and claims it "replaces 5 apps", promising "AI to turn voice recordings into action items, summarize meeting notes, and help you focus on what actually matters" (superlist.com). Positioning is a beautiful, fast, collaborative to-do list that absorbed notes and then bolted AI meeting capture on top — NOT a knowledge base and not a second brain. The centre of gravity is the shared list and the team; the AI wedge they chose is the MEETING, not the raw spoken thought. Wikipedia records the lineage: "The team responsible for creating Wunderlist, led by co-founder Christian Reber, created the Superlist app in early 2024", after Microsoft acquired Wunderlist maker 6Wunderkinder on June 1, 2015 "for between US$100 million and US$200 million" (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wunderlist).

**Maturity and evidence of use**

Company: Superlist Software GmbH (App Store seller field). Lineage verified: Wunderlist's team, led by co-founder Christian Reber; Microsoft acquired 6Wunderkinder on June 1, 2015 "for between US$100 million and US$200 million", and per Wikipedia the same team "created the Superlist app in early 2024" (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wunderlist). Funding amounts and investors: UNVERIFIED — superlist.com/about returns 404 and no primary funding page was fetchable. iOS app first released 2023-11-23, so roughly 2 years 9 months in market as of today. Current version 1.57.1, released 2026-08-25 — six days ago, so shipping actively. App Store US: 4.51 stars from 446 ratings (itunes.apple.com/lookup?id=1547585270, retrieved 2026-08-31). That rating count is the headline number: 446 versus Craft's 6,597 — Superlist has roughly 1/15th of Craft's US review base despite far heavier funding and pedigree. Bundle is 134.6MB. Product Hunt: 4.5/5 from 27 reviews. AlternativeTo: 4.3/5 from just 6 ratings. Localisation is thin: the App Store lists only EN, DE, ES — no Dutch UI, even though Talk transcribes Dutch and Flemish.

**Platforms**

iOS (min iOS 15.0, v1.57.1, apps.apple.com id1547585270) · Android (play.google.com/store/apps/details?id=com.superlist.superlist — listing content not retrievable, rating/downloads unverified) · macOS (direct download via storage.googleapis.com/superlist-appcast; meeting notes require macOS 14+) · Web (app.superlist.com) · No Apple Watch, no lock-screen voice capture documented

**Pricing**

Three tiers, all per-person, from superlist.com/pricing: (1) Free — $0/person/month, "free forever": up to 5 private and shared lists, up to 5 people in shared lists, unlimited tasks and notes, macOS/iOS/Android/Web, 25MB uploads and 500MB storage. (2) Basic — $5/person/month or $59/year: unlimited lists, sublists and shared lists with team and guests, all integrations, unlimited team members, "Voice AI for tasks", 100MB uploads and 25GB storage. (3) Super — $21/person/month or $249/year: unlimited AI Meeting Notes, unlimited AI Chat messages, "AI generation with Make AI", email + Slack task summarization. Currency symbol shown is $; the entity is Superlist Software GmbH (Germany), so EU VAT handling is unverified. IMPORTANT DISCREPANCY: the help centre is stale and still refers to a "Pro plan" that no longer appears on the pricing page. help.superlist.com/en/articles/74945 says "Talk is available as part of our Pro plan"; help.superlist.com/en/articles/310871 gives meeting caps of "Up to 30 meetings/month or 15 hours/month" on Pro and unlimited on Super; help.superlist.com/en/articles/314879 says "AI Chat with Meeting Notes is available to Pro Plan subscribers with up to 15 messages per day, and unlimited messages for Super Plan subscribers." Mapping Pro→Basic is my inference — unverified. AlternativeTo summarises the range as "$5–$25 monthly" (alternativeto.net/software/superlist/about). Key commercial fact for us: voice is NOT in the free tier. A 1-star reviewer complains exactly about this — "Main features require purchase", noting the text-to-tasks AI needs a paid plan (App Store reviews RSS, v1.55.3). Funding amount and investors: UNVERIFIED — I could not fetch a primary source; superlist.com/about is a 404 and the blog carries no company history.

**Capture UX**

Voice capture is called Talk. Entry points, quoted from help.superlist.com/en/articles/224824: on iOS/Android "Long press on the plus button will start Talk immediately"; there is a Talk button in the bottom bar of the Inbox and Today views, and a Talk button next to "+New Task" inside an individual list. The recording flow: "As soon as you start talking, you'll see the voice recording collecting your ideas. If you want to take a moment, you can pause and resume. Press the checkmark button and Talk AI will generate actionable task titles based on your input." So: pause/resume supported, and the user must explicitly confirm with a checkmark — it is a modal, foreground, in-app recorder, not a one-tap fire-and-forget. Desktop Talk availability is not documented (unverified). CRITICAL LIMITATION: capture is context-bound, not global — output lands in whatever surface you launched from (Inbox, Today, or the specific list). Audio notes are more restricted still: per help.superlist.com/en/articles/74945 the audio-note behaviour "only works in lists, not in the Inbox or Today view." No recording length limit is stated anywhere I fetched (help.superlist.com/en/articles/227277 covers phrasing only). NO lock-screen capture, NO Apple Watch, NO home-screen widget voice capture, NO share-sheet voice, NO offline voice are documented — the marketing site does advertise "Home screen and lock screen widgets" and offline support generally (superlist.com), but never for Talk. Typed quick capture is stronger: Command+K or Shift+Option+Space opens a quick command from anywhere (help.superlist.com/en/articles/23853), and the store listing promises natural-language dates — "type 'call Sarah tomorrow at 3pm' and Superlist handles the rest." Meeting capture is a separate surface: per help.superlist.com/en/articles/310871 the app "will allow you to start a Meeting Summary at anytime and use your audio to listen to the meeting" — device-level audio capture, not a bot that joins the call — covering Zoom, Google Meet, Microsoft Teams, Slack Huddles and Discord, with Google Calendar triggering automatic notifications. Requires macOS 14+ or the iOS app.

**Processing and AI**

Two distinct AI pipelines. (1) Talk: speech is transcribed and an LLM "will generate actionable task titles based on your input" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/224824). It can split ONE recording into MULTIPLE tasks — the documented example is "I want to update my website. I have to create a mood board, define a visual direction, and contact my development agency for a quote" producing several tasks — and it supports what the docs call "Multi Tasks", a parent task with subtasks, plus due-date extraction from phrases like "tomorrow" or "4:00 pm" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/74945). The docs frame it as "like sending WhatsApp voice notes to your personal assistant." (2) Meeting Notes: produces a live-readable transcript, "clean summaries", "key decisions", and a "ready-to-do list of next actions" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/310871). Plus AI Chat over meetings: "Ask Superlist's AI anything about your past meetings — what was decided, who agreed to do what, what was discussed — and get instant, sourced answers from the transcript" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/314879). Languages: 37 listed for Talk, and Dutch and Flemish are listed as SEPARATE entries alongside Bulgarian, Catalan, Chinese (Mandarin Simplified/Traditional, Cantonese Traditional), Czech, Danish, English, Estonian, Finnish, French, German, German (Switzerland), Greek, Hindi, Hungarian, Indonesian, Italian, Japanese, Korean, Latvian, Lithuanian, Malay, Norwegian, Polish, Portuguese, Romanian, Russian, Slovak, Spanish, Swedish, Thai, Turkish, Ukrainian, Vietnamese (help.superlist.com/en/articles/227377). The doc adds: "If you use one of these languages for voice input via Talk, the generated text will use the same language." That split of Dutch vs Flemish plus German (Switzerland) is the signature of Deepgram's language list — vendor UNVERIFIED, no subprocessor is named. Privacy claims are thin: "We never store audio files or transcripts without your consent" and "Your data is not shared or used for training purposes" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/227395); no retention period and no model vendor is disclosed. On-device processing: none claimed — assume cloud. THE MOST IMPORTANT FINDING FOR US is the best-practices page, which quietly admits Talk is a dictation-shaped command interface rather than a thought-capture interface: "Talk like you're typing" — use structured speech "as if you're typing into Superlist, rather than loosely speaking"; "Be specific"; "Know what you want" before using Talk; "Maintain sequential flow: Start with topic A, move to B, and then C" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/227277). They are training the user to speak like a form. There is NO confidence gate, NO intent classification beyond task-extraction, NO auto-categorisation, NO project matching and NO people-suggestion from a voice capture documented anywhere.

**Data model**

ENTITIES. (1) List — the top-level container and the de facto project. Lists nest: "Create a list within a list to structure your projects and nest information" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/22661). Lists carry cover images and emojis, and are the sharing boundary (members and guests are invited per list). Free tier caps you at 5 private + shared lists. (2) Block stream inside a list — a list holds an ordered mix of tasks, paragraph/text blocks (bold, italics, hyperlinks, strikethrough), H1/H2/H3 headings, dividers, sublists, images and file attachments up to 25MB. Quote: "Capture tasks, notes, images, and discussions in one place." (3) Task — a block type, NOT a separate table. Attributes documented: title, due date, label, assignee, subtasks with a progress indicator, attachments, an in-task discussion/chat thread, a repeat rule (v1.57.1 added exact weekdays), and an Activity Log — "Open any task to see who changed what, and when" (App Store release notes, v1.57.1). (4) Task detail page — the crux. "You can add tasks, texts, and inline images to a task details page" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/23853). ANSWERING THE KEY QUESTION: Superlist does NOT unify a note and a task into one object with a type flag. It has no Note entity at all. A "note" is simply a text block — either a paragraph sitting in a list's block stream, or a paragraph inside a task's detail page. A task is a block that additionally carries a checkbox and scheduling metadata and can expand into its own nested block stream. The unification is structural (one block stream, many block types) rather than semantic (one row, one action_type column). This is the opposite of our design: we have ONE actions table with action_type discriminating note/task/reminder/message/question/statement/research; they have one document tree where checkbox-ness is a property of a line. Consequence: they cannot ask "what kind of thing is this?" because the answer is decided by which block type the user (or Talk) picked at creation time — there is no way to later reclassify a note as a task other than editing the block. (5) Inbox — NOT a container, a STATE/flag on a task that is orthogonal to which list it lives in. "By default, your inbox contains tasks that require your attention, like tasks assigned to you, tasks added from integrations, and tasks added via quick command" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/73472). It leaves by three routes: "Mark a task as complete", "Right-click (or long press) on a task in your inbox and select Remove from inbox", or "Drag and drop a task onto a list." This IS a real unprocessed/triage state, and it is closer to our Inbox than Craft's is — but note it is triage-by-attention (assigned to me, arrived from an integration), NOT triage-by-AI-confidence. Nothing routes to the Inbox because the model was unsure. (6) Today — a purely derived view: "all the tasks due today", "any tasks that are overdue", plus Google Calendar meetings if connected. "Any tasks you've created in the Inbox or in lists will automatically appear in Today, whether they're assigned to you or not" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/78868). (7) People — exist only as workspace/list members, guests, and the task assignee field. There is no standalone Person entity with roles or history, and nothing analogous to our people M2M with roles. Talk does not suggest people. (8) Meeting note — materialised as A NEW LIST: the docs say meeting notes are "created" as "a new list" (help.superlist.com/en/articles/310871). So a meeting is not its own entity; the transcript, summary and next actions are poured into the generic list/block container. Whether the extracted "next actions" become real Task blocks or stay as summary text is NOT clarified in the docs — unverified, and it is the single most important unknown about their model. (9) Labels — a flat label field on a task. There are no tags, no backlinks and no graph; Product Hunt reviewers explicitly ask for "better tags, backlinks" (producthunt.com/products/superlist/reviews). NO Project entity, NO category enum, NO suggested_* shadow columns, NO confidence score, NO research-session entity, NO capture entity holding audio + transcript as a first-class row (audio notes attach inside lists only). HOW A CAPTURE BECOMES A TASK: Talk transcribes → LLM writes task titles → task blocks are inserted directly into the surface you were standing in. There is no intermediate proposal object, no review step, no accept/reject, and no record of what the AI guessed versus what the user corrected.

**Organisation and retrieval**

Organisation is manual and hierarchical: nested lists and sublists, headings inside lists, a flat label per task, manual drag-to-sort, and cover images/emoji for visual identification. Retrieval surfaces are Inbox, Today, and per-list views, plus sort/filter and hide-completed (help.superlist.com/en/articles/78871) and a cross-list "View and sort all tasks" article exists (help.superlist.com/en/articles/10058). Search quality is a repeated complaint — Product Hunt reviewers cite "Missing bulk editing, better tags, backlinks, search functionality." There is no graph, no backlinks, no daily note, and no AI-driven auto-filing. The one AI retrieval surface is scoped narrowly to meetings (AI Chat over past calls), not to the whole workspace. So the AI helps you get things IN (Talk) and interrogate meetings, but it does not help you find or re-organise anything else.

**Outputs and integrations**

Integrations (help.superlist.com/en/collections/2736): Gmail ("turn emails into tasks"), Google Calendar (see tasks alongside events, and it triggers meeting-notes notifications), Slack ("turn messages into tasks"), GitHub (issues and PRs), Linear (issues as tasks), email forwarding into Superlist, and Microsoft To Do two-way sync. The store listing adds Figma, Notion and Loom to the claimed list (alternativeto.net/software/superlist/about). Super tier adds "Email + Slack task summarization" and "AI generation with Make AI" (superlist.com/pricing). Collaboration outputs: real-time shared lists, per-task assignment, per-task chat threads, activity log. There is no publishing, no public sharing of a document, no API documented in the help centre (unverified), and no export path documented — a real lock-in risk they do not advertise. GitHub org exists at github.com/superlistapp.

**Strengths**

- Voice is a first-class capture surface with a genuinely good gesture: long-press the + button starts recording immediately, with pause/resume and an explicit checkmark to commit — this is the closest thing on the market to our core interaction and we should treat it as the bar to beat.
- One recording reliably fans out into MULTIPLE tasks, including a parent-with-subtasks structure, rather than dumping one blob. This is the behaviour users actually want from a brain dump.
- Dutch AND Flemish are listed as separate supported languages for Talk — they have already validated our first market's language, which removes the 'is this even possible' risk for us.
- Meeting capture is device-audio based, not a bot joining the call, covering Zoom, Meet, Teams, Slack Huddles and Discord — far less friction and no awkward third participant.
- The Inbox is a real triage state decoupled from location, with three clean exits (complete, remove from inbox, drag to a list). The mental model is clean and worth copying.
- Task detail pages hold arbitrary blocks, so a task can carry a whole note without a second object — no 'which app does this live in' decision.
- Generous free tier on the fundamentals: unlimited tasks and notes, 5 lists, 5 collaborators.
- Real-time collaboration, per-task chat and an activity log give them a genuine team story we do not have.
- Deep, practical integration set (Gmail, Slack, Linear, GitHub, Google Calendar, Microsoft To Do) that feeds the Inbox automatically.

**Weaknesses**

- THE BIG ONE: their own best-practices doc instructs users to speak like a form — "Talk like you're typing", "as if you're typing into Superlist, rather than loosely speaking", "Know what you want" before using Talk. They have voice input but not voice THINKING. A rambling, half-formed, unstructured thought is explicitly out of scope. That is the entire gap Delta exists to fill.
- Voice is paywalled — 'Voice AI for tasks' starts at $5/person/month and the free tier has none. A 1-star App Store review titled "Main features require purchase" attacks exactly this. Anyone who wants to try voice-first capture must pay before experiencing the magic.
- Capture is context-bound, not global: Talk output lands wherever you launched it, and audio notes "only works in lists, not in the Inbox or Today view". You have to decide where a thought goes BEFORE you have the thought — the exact friction we are removing.
- No lock-screen, widget, Watch, or share-sheet voice capture documented. Everything requires unlocking the phone and opening the app.
- No AI classification layer at all: no intent detection, no category proposal, no project matching, no people suggestion, no confidence score. Everything the AI produces is committed immediately with no proposal/review step and no record of the guess.
- No Project entity — a list is the project, so there is no summary, no cross-list rollup, and no way for AI to match a capture to an existing project.
- No Person entity beyond list membership and an assignee field; nothing tracks who a thought was about.
- Meeting output is dumped into a generic new list; whether extracted next actions become real tasks is undocumented, which suggests the seam between 'AI wrote this' and 'this is a real task' is unresolved even for them.
- Weak retrieval: no tags (only a flat label), no backlinks, no daily note, and reviewers explicitly flag search as missing.
- Maturity complaints are consistent and old: Product Hunt reviewers say it "launched as an unfinished product" and "is not usable in its current state. It lacks rudimentary keyboard actions"; App Store reviewers report iPad freezes (v1.57.0), missing calendar and snooze (v1.55.0), and "no keyboard shortcuts to change format" (v1.54.0).
- Only 446 US ratings after nearly three years, from the Wunderlist team with funding. Pedigree plus money has not produced traction — sobering evidence that 'notes + tasks + AI' is not automatically a winning pitch.
- Help centre is stale: it still documents a "Pro plan" that the pricing page no longer sells, and the meeting caps (30 meetings/15 hours per month) cannot be reliably mapped to a current tier.
- Only EN/DE/ES UI — no Dutch interface for our first market despite Dutch transcription support.
- Privacy disclosure is thin: no named subprocessor, no retention period, and audio storage is gated on a vague notion of "consent".

**Ideas for Delta**

- Steal the long-press-the-plus-to-record gesture outright, but make ours GLOBAL and context-free: our confidence gate means the user never has to pick a destination first. Their best-practices page is our marketing copy — we should be able to say 'ramble; we'll sort it', which they explicitly tell users not to do.
- Put voice in the FREE tier. Their $5 paywall on the one magic interaction is a self-inflicted wound and the clearest commercial opening. Charge for volume, history, or projects — never for the first capture.
- Position explicitly against 'talk like you're typing'. Our one-line differentiator: Superlist wants a dictated command; Delta accepts a half-formed thought and does the structuring. This is a demonstrable, screenshot-able difference, not a claim.
- Multi-item extraction from one recording is table stakes — they already do it, including parent/subtask. Our understand step must at minimum split one capture into several actions, or we look worse than the incumbent on day one.
- Adopt their Inbox semantics: a state on the entity, not a container, with explicit exits. But make ours confidence-driven (below 0.75 parks it) rather than attention-driven — that is a genuinely new reason for something to be in an inbox, and it is defensible.
- Our suggested_* shadow columns are a real architectural advantage neither product has. Nobody keeps the AI's guess separate from the confirmed value, which means nobody can learn from corrections. Build the correction-feedback loop early — it is a moat they cannot retrofit cheaply.
- Ship a Dutch AND Flemish UI, not just Dutch transcription. They transcribe Flemish but ship no Dutch interface. A Flemish-language voice app for the Belgian market is an uncontested niche.
- Copy the transcript-plus-sourced-answers pattern ("instant, sourced answers from the transcript") for our research sessions with sources — citing back into the original capture builds trust in AI output cheaply.
- Keep raw audio attached to the capture unconditionally. Superlist restricts audio notes to lists only, which is an arbitrary limit users will hit; our captures table already holds audio + transcript together, so make replay available everywhere.
- Their meeting-notes-become-a-list fudge shows what happens without a proper entity model. Our actions table with action_type is the cleaner answer — but only if we resist the temptation to add a Meeting entity later. Keep the discriminator, not new tables.
- Do NOT chase team collaboration. Their real-time shared lists, guests, per-task chat and activity log are a large surface we cannot match, and it is the part of their product that dilutes the capture story. Solo-first is a strategic choice, not a limitation.
- Warning on traction: 446 ratings in three years for a funded Wunderlist team means distribution, not features, is the hard problem. Budget effort for reaching Flemish/Dutch users accordingly, and do not assume a better model wins by itself.
