## Appendix: raw sweep notes

Free-form notes from the four wave-1 discovery agents, kept because they contain trend observations the structured schema could not hold.

```text
voice-ai: SCOPE AND METHOD. 24 web searches plus ~16 direct page fetches; the session's search budget ran out before I could verify two remaining threads (Genspark Secondbrain / Mobvoi TicNote device traction, and a confirmation sweep on dead apps like Dot by New Computer and Airchat). Wherever I could, I verified ratings and review counts by fetching the live Apple App Store page rather than trusting a blog; those are marked "verified" in evidenceOfUse. Google Play pages would not render through WebFetch and AppBrain returned 403, so Android numbers come from secondary sources and are weaker. Anything I could not confirm is labelled unverified in-field, as instructed.

THE BIG STRUCTURAL FINDING FOR DELTA. This category has split into three tiers that are converging on each other, and Delta currently sits in the gap between them. (1) The dictation tier — Wispr Flow at $2B, Willow, Aqua, superwhisper, Monologue — is where essentially all the venture money went in 2025-26. It has commoditised "speech to clean text" and is now growing upward into notes. (2) The voice-notes tier — Voicenotes, AudioPen, Cleft, TalkNotes, Audionotes, SpeakApp — is almost entirely bootstrapped, tops out in the $5K-$100K/mo range, and adds cleanup and summary but almost no persistent structure. (3) The structured-knowledge tier — Tana, Capacities, Mem, Reflect — has the entity model Delta wants but treats voice as a feature bolted onto typing. Nobody I found is doing all three well: voice-first capture, inferred intent, AND a typed entity graph with a review gate. That is a real gap, but note the asymmetry — the funded tier is coming for it from a position of strength.

CLOSEST DIRECT ANALOGUES, RANKED. Tana (typed supertag objects + voice memos into the graph) is the closest on data model. Voicenotes and Cleft are closest on capture UX. Saner.ai is closest on the one-input-fans-out-to-task-or-note-or-reminder behaviour. Sandbar's Stream ring is closest on interaction philosophy (deliberate push-to-talk, not ambient). Delta's genuinely differentiated pieces versus all of them are the confidence gate that decides auto-file versus Inbox, and the People/Contacts dimension — I did not find a consumer voice app that proposes people as first-class entities from a spoken thought.

WHAT KILLED PRODUCTS IN THIS CATEGORY. Two recurring causes, both worth internalising. First, capture reliability, not AI quality: Voicenotes' App Store reviews are dominated by recordings truncating silently, Cleft's entire 2.0 rewrite is framed around "dependable offline capture", and Mem needed a ground-up rebuild after years of speed and sync complaints. Nobody churns because the summary was mediocre; they churn because a thought was lost. Second, the retrieval half: Limitless reviewers consistently reported that capture worked and recall/organisation did not, and Meta ultimately bought the team rather than the product. Delta's "stays findable" promise is the harder half of its own pitch, and it is the half the market has repeatedly failed at.

DEAD, ABSORBED, OR PIVOTED (all included at relevance 1-2, per the brief). Limitless/Rewind — acquired by Meta 5 Dec 2025, pendant pulled from sale, Rewind desktop wound down, users moved to a free plan with support through 2026. Bee — acquired by Amazon (agreed July 2025), still sold, now an Amazon Devices product. Humane AI Pin — fully dead, cloud shut off 28 Feb 2025, assets to HP for $116M after fewer than 10,000 units on ~$230M raised. Oasis — did not die but pivoted away from exactly Delta's product shape into the TalkTastic dictation keyboard; its marketing site now returns almost no content and 2026 third-party reviews still describe a free Mac beta, so I would treat it as stalled. Rabbit R1 — still shipping as of Aug 2026 but reportedly struggling; excluded as it is not a notes product. Coconote's App Store listing now shows Quizlet Inc as developer, implying an acquisition I could not confirm.

EXCLUDED AS NOT ACTUALLY USED — this is a category full of them. Search results for "voice note AI app 2026" are heavily polluted by two things. First, near-zero-usage App Store listings that look plausible in a roundup: I fetched VoiceTask AI (id6753657106, developer Jan Horak, "not yet rated" — insufficient ratings to show a score) and Voice Inbox AI (id6760556777, developer Mikita Kandratsyeu, 5.0 from exactly 1 rating). Both describe almost precisely Delta's feature set — voice in, tasks/notes/reminders/projects out — and both have effectively no users. Delta's idea is not novel; execution and reliability are the whole game. Second, SEO content farms that rank by publishing "best AI voice notes app 2026" comparisons naming themselves first: speakwiseapp.com, lumanote.org, voicetonotes.ai, spokenly.app, getvoibe.com, weesperneonflow.ai, remi8.ai, yaps.ai, ainotely.com, voicebase.app. I verified one of them properly — Speakwise has 55 App Store ratings yet appears in a large share of top search results — and included it at relevance 2 for that lesson alone. I could not verify real usage for the others and excluded them. Treat any 2026 "best voice note app" roundup as marketing until the app-store numbers are checked directly.

TWO FIGURES I DISTRUST AND FLAGGED IN-LINE. The App Store fetch returned "last update August 15, 2024" for Monologue, which cannot be right for an app that launched September 2025; and superwhisper's pricing came back as "$849/month", which is a misparse of $8.49. Both are noted as unverified in their entries. Also note two App Store listings whose last-update dates suggest possible staleness: Voicepal (July 2024) and the Talknotes listing under developer "Collin Brewer" (May 2025) — for the latter I could not confirm it is even the same product as talknotes.io.

MONETISATION REFERENCE POINTS. Subscriptions cluster at $6.99-$14.99/mo, with Cleft recently cutting its annual from $89 to $39 — a signal of price pressure at the indie end. Lifetime and one-time pricing is common and works (AudioPen ~$99/yr with cheaper passes, superwhisper $249.99 lifetime, MacWhisper ~€59 one-time on roughly 300K copies). Hardware players monetise the structuring layer, not the recording: Plaud gives 300 free minutes then charges, Pocket sells at $129 hardware plus $200/yr. The strategic read is that transcription has no price left in it; the structure and the retrieval do.
stores: STORE-CHART FINDING (the most important one). I fetched the live US iPhone Productivity chart (apps.apple.com/us/charts/iphone/productivity-apps/6007), the US Utilities chart (.../utilities-apps/6002) and the German Productivity chart (apps.apple.com/de/charts/iphone/produktivitat-apps/6007) in Aug 2026. Not one dedicated voice, dictation or transcription app appears in the top 25 of any of them. The top of both US and German Productivity is ChatGPT, Google Gemini and Claude, then authenticators, mail and cloud storage. Utilities is browsers, VPNs and cleaners. So the category we are entering has real, monetising products but zero chart presence - discovery is not going to come from browsing the store. The best-ranked voice apps I found are Wispr Flow at #61 and Otter at #58 in US Productivity, Granola at #84, Aiko at #45, Plaud at #123, and Just Press Record at #8 in paid Utilities.

METHOD AND RELIABILITY. Apple App Store pages fetch cleanly and give rating, rating count, category rank, price and last-update date - that is where nearly all my hard numbers come from, read directly off the listings in Aug 2026. Google Play pages are NOT fetchable through this tool (they return a truncated shell every time, and AppBrain returns 403 on direct fetch and androidrank 404s), so all Android numbers here come from AppBrain figures surfaced in search snippets or from APKCombo's structured data, and are estimates rather than store-reported. Sensor Tower, Appfigures and SimilarWeb writeups were not reachable. Where I could not verify something I said so in the field rather than guessing.

RATING COUNTS ARE THE HONEST FILTER. There is a very large gap between what the AI-note blogosphere ranks and what has real users. Almost every "best voice notes app 2026" listicle I hit was content marketing published by a competitor (speakwiseapp.com, voicetonotes.ai, lumanote.org, spokenly.app, notelyn.com, getvoibe.com, basilai.app, fluidvox.com all publish rankings that place themselves at or near the top). I ignored all of those rankings and used store data instead. On store data the ordering is: Apple Voice Memos 1.1M ratings, Otter 76K, Plaud 22K, Wispr Flow 15K, Granola 13K, Wave 12K, Drafts 11K, Voicenotes 6.7K, Just Press Record 1.5K, Notta 1.4K, superwhisper 820, Voicepal 648, Aiko 325, AudioPen 154 iOS, Audionotes 91, Cleft 68, Talknotes 52, Tana 50, Braintoss 47. Several apps positioned online as major players are in the double digits of ratings.

VENDOR CLAIMS I COULD NOT VERIFY, flagged rather than dropped: Audionotes' "4.9+ by 200K+ users" (its verified iOS listing has 91 ratings); Talknotes' "10,000+ professionals"; Fireflies' "20 million users" (its own paying-customer figure is ~35K); Hedy's "#1 AI meeting assistant 2026" award, which came from a vendor-issued GlobeNewswire release; Otter's 2026 revenue, where sources conflict badly ($100M ARR per Otter's own Mar 2025 release vs a $22.3M 2026 figure on an aggregator).

DEAD OR ABSORBED (all marked relevance 1-2). Limitless Pendant - Meta acquisition 5 Dec 2025, hardware sales ended, Rewind wound down, users moved to a free plan supported through 2026. Airchat - shut down in practice, sunset of the current version announced Mar 2026. Bee - acquired by Amazon Jul 2025. Mem - no shutdown evidence but no funding since Nov 2022 and no verifiable current traction. That is four of the most-hyped names in this space in two years, three of them absorbed by big tech. The consolidation pattern is: consumer ambient-capture startups do not scale to independent businesses; they get bought for the team.

THE ONE COUNTEREXAMPLE THAT MATTERS: Plaud. It shipped 2M+ devices, crossed $100M ARR on software, is profitable, took no venture money for most of that run, and is targeting $500M in 2026 sales (TechCrunch and Bloomberg, both 16 Jun 2026). It is the only company in this sweep that built a large independent business on voice capture, and it did it by removing the phone from the capture step and selling hardware. Worth weighing against the assumption that the app alone is the product.

THREE TRENDS THAT CONSTRAIN DELTA'S POSITIONING.
1. Transcription is now free and built into the OS. Apple Notes does live transcript plus AI summary in 10 languages, Voice Memos transcribes on iPhone 12+, Samsung ships Transcript Assist on every Galaxy AI flagship in 20+ languages, Google Recorder does it offline on Pixel. Nobody will pay for transcription in 2026. The chargeable layer is what happens to the thought afterwards.
2. Organisation is the unmet need, and it is visible in the reviews of the market leader. Voicenotes' App Store reviews - 4.8 stars, 6.7K ratings - are dominated by complaints about note categorisation and the inability to separate notes by project, alongside reliability. That is Delta's thesis validated by a competitor's own users. Reliability is the other lesson: multiple high-rated voice apps take their heaviest criticism for recordings that stop mid-session, so capture robustness matters more than model quality.
3. Language coverage is a real European opening. Apple's Voice Memos transcription is English-locale-only. Apple Notes adds nine more languages but not Dutch. Google Recorder is Pixel-only. For a Dutch or Belgian user, the free OS options are materially worse than they are for an American, and the German App Store chart shows no local voice app filling that gap.

SEARCH DEAD-ENDS worth knowing: no reachable Sensor Tower or Appfigures download estimates for this category; no AppBrain page for Google Recorder; Play Store install counts unobtainable by direct fetch; and searching product names like "Voicenotes", "Wave" or "Voice Notes AI" returns a swarm of near-identical copycat listings with nearly the same name and icon (I found at least five distinct App Store apps called some variant of "Voicenotes"), which makes name collision a genuine practical risk for Delta's eventual rename.
quick-capture: SEARCH METHOD: ~24 web searches plus direct fetches of App Store listings (Drafts, Things, Todoist, TickTick, Twos, Braintoss, Obsidian, Tana, Cleft, Voicenotes), vendor pages (getdrafts.com, audiopen.ai, Capacities docs) and press (TechCrunch, Bloomberg via search summaries). Ratings and review counts marked as App Store figures were read off the listing page directly.

WHAT THE INBOX/TRIAGE MECHANICS ACTUALLY REDUCE TO — five distinct patterns, which is the useful frame for designing our confidence gate:
1. Explicit inbox + manual triage verbs (Drafts: Tag/Flag/Archive; Todoist/Things: Inbox → project; Evernote: default notebook). Reliable, but triage is a chore the user must schedule.
2. Daily-note-as-inbox (Logseq, Roam, Amplenote Jots, NotePlan, Capacities, Twos). No filing decision at capture; the date does the filing. Triage is a review ritual, or a rollover that forces it daily. Capacities documents this best: two filters, "do I want to remember this?" at capture, "is this useful now?" at weekly review.
3. Parse-and-commit, no inbox (Todoist Quick Add, Fantastical, TickTick). The structure is extracted at entry and shown inline for correction *before* commit. This is the strongest UI pattern I found and it is cheaper than a gate: correction is free while the user is still looking at it, expensive once it's filed.
4. Abolish triage entirely (mymind, Mem, Voicenotes, Google Keep). Everything is kept, AI tags it, retrieval is search. mymind's users praise search, not organization — which suggests our success metric is retrieval quality more than filing accuracy. Mem needed a 2.0 rebuild; Voicenotes' top complaint is invented to-dos and lost notes. This is the failure mode our gate exists to prevent, and it is real, not hypothetical.
5. Outsource the inbox (Braintoss → email; Cleft → Notion/Obsidian/Drafts; Slack Later tab; Telegram Saved Messages). Deliberately does not create a second inbox. Worth taking seriously: if Delta's Inbox becomes another place to check, we recreate the problem.

DECLARE-TYPE-BEFORE-SPEAKING: Tana is the one funded product doing our understand step on voice, and it sidesteps classification risk by having the user attach a supertag (schema) at capture; the AI then only fills fields, it doesn't guess intent. That is a viable alternative design to a confidence gate and should be grilled against ours.

VOICE QUALITY, HONESTLY: in the quick-capture tradition proper (Drafts, Things, Bear, NotePlan, Obsidian, Amplenote, Todoist, Google Keep, Apple Notes) voice input is almost always just the OS dictation engine — fine for one sentence, poor for a 60–90 second unstructured ramble, and none of them do anything with the audio beyond inserting text. The only ones with genuine voice-native capture are Google Keep (records + auto-transcribes, keeps audio), Tana (server transcription + supertag structuring), Capacities (WhatsApp/Telegram voice notes become audio objects on the daily note), TickTick (2026 AI voice mode — third-party sourced, unverified), and the voice-first tier (Voicenotes, AudioPen, Cleft, Whisper Memos). That gap between "the good inbox apps have bad voice" and "the good voice apps have no inbox" is the clearest whitespace this research surfaced, and it is exactly where Delta sits.

DEAD / DYING / ABSORBED (all marked relevance 1 or 2): Limitless Pendant — acquired by Meta 5 Dec 2025, no longer sold to new customers, support ~through late 2026. Bee — acquired by Amazon 22 July 2025, folded into the Alexa/Echo roadmap. Roam Research — not shut down but in visible decline, dominant 2026 coverage is exit guides. Logseq — not abandoned but slowed; DB version still beta as of July 2026 with mobile and sync in alpha, and a real migration narrative. Cleft — shipping and well-reviewed but the App Store page showed a July 2024 version date, so its current cadence is unverified and it may be stalling. Evernote — alive and shipping under Bending Spoons but shedding users to a price increase and a crippled free tier.

FUNDING SIGNAL: the money in 2026 is in the input layer, not the inbox. Wispr Flow raised $280M at $2B in August 2026 (total $361M). Plaud crossed $100M software ARR having shipped 2M+ devices, entirely bootstrapped, targeting $500M sales in 2026. Otter is at $100M ARR with 25M+ users. By contrast the quick-capture-inbox tradition is almost entirely small indie or bootstrapped businesses (Drafts, Braintoss, Twos, Amplenote, NotePlan, Cleft, AudioPen, mymind), with Todoist (~$20M ARR, bootstrapped) as the biggest. Tana's $25M is the only significant venture money aimed at the structuring-a-spoken-thought problem specifically.

PLATFORM RISK, NAMED: Microsoft shipped multimodal Capture for Copilot Notebooks into OneNote (Windows July 2026, Android preview August 2026) which turns recorded audio into structured notes with decisions and action items. Apple's Action-button-to-Quick-Note plus Siri is the free floor everyone competes against. TickTick's April 2026 8.x releases reportedly added AI voice input and audio transcription with summaries. Our differentiator cannot be capture speed or transcription — those are commoditized or bundled. It has to be the entity model (projects, people, actions) plus retrieval.

THINGS I COULD NOT VERIFY: Bear's App Store rating count and current Pro price; Amplenote's site (403 on both / and /pricing — pricing tiers come from third-party aggregators, treat $7/$12/$25 as approximate); NotePlan's exact rating count and current subscription price; Reflect and superwhisper were dropped as separate entries because I could not confirm their official URLs in-session (Reflect: ~$10/mo billed annually, single plan, Whisper transcription, no free tier; superwhisper: 4.4 App Store, $8.49/mo or $249.99 lifetime, bootstrapped Toronto company, on-device Whisper/Parakeet models — all from third-party reviews only). Braintoss's claimed May 2026 update / "Braintoss 10 native rebuild" conflicts with the June 2024 version date on the App Store page I fetched. Google Keep's 4B downloads and 1.2M/day are AppBrain third-party estimates, not Google figures (the AppBrain page itself returned 403 on direct fetch). Telegram's ~1B monthly users is from general knowledge, not verified this session. One source made a claim about a Tana "December 2026" update, which is in the future — I discarded that source's specifics as unreliable.

SEARCH DEAD-ENDS: the 2026 SEO layer around productivity apps is heavily polluted — dozens of near-identical "X Review 2026 / Best X Alternatives 2026" pages, many of them written by competing voice-note startups (speakwiseapp, getvoibe, spokenly, yaps, weesperneonflow all appeared as "review" sources for their own competitors). I treated those as leads only and confirmed numbers against App Store listings and named press (TechCrunch, Bloomberg, ArcticStartup, SiliconANGLE) wherever possible. Momentum-style new-tab widgets and Slackbot-to-self were checked but neither is a product with independent evidence of use as a capture tool — Slack's own Later tab is documented and included instead.
reviews: SEARCH CONSTRAINTS AND METHOD. Reddit is hard-blocked for this agent's web fetcher (the API refuses reddit.com in allowed_domains and reddit results are filtered out of general searches), so the demand-side reading came from other user-voice sources instead: App Store and Play Store review bodies, Product Hunt review pages, Trustpilot, G2, Indie Hackers, and independent long-form tester write-ups. Two of those were high-signal and worth reading directly: fourhourfreedom.substack.com/p/i-tested-every-voice-recorder-app (tested Otter, AudioPen, Whisper Memos and a DIY Apple Voice Memos + Whisper pipeline, settled on Tana) and nerdymomocat.github.io/posts/voicenotes-hitting-half-of-the-right-notes (Voicenotes vs AudioPen vs TalkNote vs Google Recorder vs Apple Voice Memos, from a PKM user's perspective). The web search budget (200 calls) was exhausted before I could re-verify a handful of official URLs; those are flagged as unverified in the relevant entries (Aqua Voice, Braintoss, Twos, Bee).

BEWARE THE SEO LAYER. This category has an unusually thick crust of fake-looking comparison content. A large share of "best voice notes app 2026" roundups are published by tiny apps ranking themselves first — speakwiseapp.com, yaps.ai, voicetonotes.ai, spokenly.app, getvoibe.com, weesperneonflow.ai, lumanote.org, nemosapp.com, noticky.app, braindumpnotes.app, blabby.ai, vozly, voicetask.app, whisperplan, getryse.app, heynori, thoughtmic, remi8.ai. I found no App Store rating counts, download figures, funding, or independent press for any of them. I deliberately excluded all of them rather than pad the list; treat every "2026 roundup" you read as a competitor's landing page until proven otherwise. RYSE self-describes as having "30 brutally honest beta testers" — that is the honest scale of this tier.

THE RECURRING COMPLAINT THEMES (this is the churn list, ranked by how often it appeared across independent sources):

1. Lost captures. The single most damaging complaint. Voicenotes reviewers on both Product Hunt and Trustpilot describe recordings that fail to upload and are then simply gone, with retry buttons that "NEVER work", and recordings that stop mid-session without warning. Nothing else on this list destroys trust as fast — the whole promise is "I don't have to hold this thought any more."
2. The capture-to-review gap. Users record hundreds of memos and never go back. Voicenotes reviewers: "no clear or effective way to categorize recordings", notes become "an unmanageable pile". Apple Voice Memos users describe being "out of control". This is the demand-side hole Delta is aimed at, and it is real and repeatedly stated.
3. Structure is manual, or absent. AudioPen and TalkNotes get dismissed together by PKM users for having "no personal knowledge management features". Voicenotes gets criticised for weak tagging with no auto-tagging, a useless Related Notes feature, and search that is keyword-based despite storing embeddings. Tana solves it but demands you build the schema yourself, which is why its churn reason is "steep learning curve before value".
4. Capture friction that shouldn't exist. AudioPen's 15-minute cap and — repeatedly cited — no background recording on iOS, so locking the screen kills the recording. Audionotes markets against exactly this (unlimited length, deliberate pause/resume, nothing lost before you press stop). Notion only fixed mobile background transcription in Jan 2026. If Delta's recorder stops when the screen locks, nothing else matters.
5. Reliability at length. Multiple sources report degradation on 30–50 minute recordings: crashes, truncated transcripts, summaries that "sit there indefinitely without generating", inconsistent timestamps.
6. Hallucinated output. Voicenotes reviewers report the AI "incorrectly generating to-do items users never mentioned". Directly relevant: Delta's confidence gate is the mitigation, and getting the gate wrong in the auto-file direction is the failure users punish hardest.
7. Language and accent handling. Independent PKM testers say Google Recorder, Apple Voice Memos and Voicenotes all handle accents and code-switching badly. Given a Dutch/English user base that code-switches mid-sentence, this is a concrete differentiation opportunity and a concrete risk.
8. Export and lock-in. Otter's churn driver per independent testers is "how much effort it took to move notes into their existing system"; Voicenotes reviewers want bulk export and consistent file formats. Cleft's export to Notion/Obsidian/Apple Notes is repeatedly cited as a reason to trust it.
9. Price shock and billing. Otter's pricing ramp past the free tier. Superwhisper's $249 lifetime pushing users elsewhere. Voicenotes Trustpilot one-stars over a $99.99 annual renewal invoiced 74 minutes before the charge. Saner.ai's 30-message free tier being too small to evaluate.
10. Trust and continuity. Amplified by Limitless: Meta bought it, hardware sales ended, EU/UK service was killed outright with a hard data-export deadline. Combined with the privacy discomfort around always-on Bee/Amazon, "will this exist next year and can I get my data out" is now a stated purchase criterion, not a nerd concern.

STRATEGIC READ FOR DELTA. Three structural things stood out. (a) The competitive line is not "voice notes app vs voice notes app" — transcription is being commoditised from two directions at once: free and unlimited from Fathom/Apple/Google, and everywhere-at-once from Wispr Flow ($2B, dictation into any app). Delta cannot charge for the transcript; the paid surface has to be the understand-and-file step. (b) The one product whose entity model matches Delta's almost exactly — people, places, projects extracted from speech — is TwinMind, and it took the ambient always-on path, which carries the privacy and volume problems that Bee and Limitless are now demonstrating publicly. Deliberate press-to-record is a genuinely differentiated position, and the argument for it is getting stronger, not weaker. (c) The audience that repeatedly self-identifies in reviews for Cleft, Saner.ai and Voicepal is ADHD/neurodivergent and verbal thinkers, and they articulate the problem in Delta's exact terms: two failure modes, not capturing and not finding. That is the beachhead. Dead or absorbed products found: Limitless/Rewind (Meta, Dec 2025, hardware dead, EU/UK service terminated), Bee (Amazon, Jul 2025, survives as a free Amazon product), TalkNotes (sold for $200K in 2024, still running). I found no evidence that Mem.ai, Reflect or fabric.so have shut down — all three appear active with voice features in 2026 — but I also could not verify meaningful current usage figures for any of them, so I left them out of the list rather than assert traction I could not source.
```

Additional notes from the wave-2 gap-fill agents:

```text
METHOD AND LIMITS. This session's WebSearch budget (200/200) was already exhausted before I began, so everything below comes from direct WebFetch of pages I could name, plus Apple's public iTunes lookup/search/reviews-RSS endpoints. Every number in the two records above was read off a page I actually fetched on 2026-08-31 and is attributed to that page. Pages that BLOCKED me, and which therefore leave real gaps: https://support.notta.ai/hc/en-us returned 403 (so Notta's authoritative help centre, and with it the full 58-language list, per-language caveats, folder/workspace semantics, retention and deletion policy, are all UNVERIFIED); https://www.g2.com/products/notta/reviews returned 403 (no G2 rating or review count — unverified); Google Play listings for both apps returned truncated or 404 content, so I have NO Play Store ratings or install counts for either product (unverified); and https://www.notta.ai/ja 404s from the language switcher even though the switcher lists 日本語, so I could not read the Japanese marketing site directly and inferred the Japanese positioning from the JP App Store listing name and JP review RSS instead. I could not find any independent hands-on review of flomo in English or Chinese without search; the Zapier transcription roundup I fetched (published May 2018, updated April 2026) covers Rev, Alice, Castmagic, Voicenotes, Wispr Flow, Otter, Descript and Riverside but does NOT cover Notta. For flomo my independent signal is therefore limited to the CN App Store reviews RSS; for Notta it is Trustpilot plus the JP and US store reviews.

TWO FIRST-PARTY CONTRADICTIONS I could not resolve. (1) flomo voice quota: https://help.flomoapp.com/membership/pro.html states PRO "10分/条·500分/月" and MAX "30分/条·1500分/月" (minutes per month), while https://help.flomoapp.com/ai/aivoice.html states PRO "每月 1,000 次" and MAX "每月 9,999 次" (invocations per month). Both are official docs; the per-clip caps (1 min free / 10 PRO / 30 MAX) agree. (2) flomo WeChat review push: https://help.flomoapp.com/thinking/how-to-review.html describes passive review including "WeChat pushes (3 daily)", but https://help.flomoapp.com/basic/wechat-input.html documents the WeChat channel as inbound text+images only and mentions no outbound review push.

THE HEADLINE COMPARISON, since the brief framed flomo as the biggest miss. flomo: 4.93 across 51,993 CN App Store ratings (iTunes lookup, id 1552314395, 2026-08-31), six years old, and it has NEVER had a folder, a task, a person, a status field, an inbox state, or a single line of AI auto-tagging. Notta: 4.35 across 26,405 JP ratings and 4.05 across 1,398 US ratings, has folders and speaker labels and AI extraction, and its Trustpilot rating was REMOVED for fake reviews. The structural read is that flomo's rating is not despite the missing structure, it is BECAUSE of it — every feature that could disappoint is absent, and the only thing the product promises (a text box that never loses anything, and four ways of handing your past back to you) is a promise it can keep every single day for six years.

THE ONE FINDING THAT SHOULD MOVE OUR ROADMAP. Our whole architecture — suggested_* columns, the 0.75/0.45 confidence gate, the Inbox — is built on the premise that a capture must be CLASSIFIED to be findable later. flomo is a six-year, ~52,000-rating, paying-subscriber counterexample: it never classifies anything, and it solves the same problem on the retrieval side instead, with embeddings (認知地図 explicitly uses "向量化相似度") plus four scheduled/serendipitous resurfacing surfaces. Its "inbox" is not a state at all — it is (a) a derived one-tap search filter for 无标签笔记 (untagged notes), and (b) the letter I in its own published P.A.I.R doctrine, i.e. a TAG the user is told to create. Nothing in the system ever forces a note out of that state. The strategic question this raises for Delta is not "should we drop the Inbox" but "is the Inbox load-bearing, or is it a debt we invented by choosing to classify at capture time?" A cheap experiment: build the resurfacing side first (embeddings over transcripts, a Daily Review with flomo's exact settings shape — 4/8/12/16/20/24 per day, scope by all/specific/excluded/uncategorised, window all/1y/6m/3m/1m, delivered to a lock-screen widget and a push framed as a letter to yourself) and measure how many Inbox items users still bother to triage once unfiled captures come back on their own.

SECOND-ORDER OBSERVATION ON MONETISATION. flomo charges nothing for capture and everything for retrieval: Daily Review, Related Notes, Cognitive Map, Random Walk, backlinks, AI Insights and voice beyond 1 minute are all PRO/MAX (¥15/mo, ¥99/yr, ¥39/mo, ¥299/yr — verified from the CN App Store IAP list). Notta charges for capture minutes and then AGAIN for AI credits, and that second paywall is visible in its complaint pattern. For a solo-built product the flomo shape is the safer one: capture is a one-time need and a bad thing to meter, retrieval is a recurring need and a fair thing to meter. flomo also pays users free PRO days for adopting each capture route, with the LARGEST single reward (+7天) for switching on Daily Review — they subsidise the retention habit, not the signup.

BELGIUM/DUTCH BOTTOM LINE. flomo's voice is Simplified Chinese and English only, stated plainly, with no Dutch and only a vague roadmap promise — it is not a competitor in our market and will not be soon. Notta CLAIMS Dutch inside "58 talen" on its partially-translated Dutch page ("Notta ondersteunt maar liefst 58 verschillende talen voor transcriptie en vertaling") but publishes no per-language quality data anywhere I could reach, and Dutch appears in NEITHER of its two on-device language sets — the local model covers auto-detect, Simplified Chinese, English, Japanese, Korean and Cantonese; the Apple Speech Analyzer route adds German, French, Spanish, Italian, Portuguese and Traditional Chinese. Dutch is cloud-only and unverified. Its bilingual mode also requires pre-declaring a fixed language PAIR from a "10+ languages" subset, which is not how a Flemish speaker talks. NL↔EN code-switching with no configuration, and a published honest per-language quality statement, is a wedge that neither of these two products currently occupies.
```

```text
THE QUESTION YOU ASKED: can a capture product succeed when captures are NEVER turned into tasks? Answer: yes, but only by changing what "success" is bought with, and the mechanism does not transfer to us.

Both products succeed on a promise that has nothing to do with retrieval quality: "this will still exist in ten years, and it will be yours." Day One's homepage sells preservation and encryption; the reviews that matter are people writing about a dead spouse's photos and a decade of entries. That is a moat made of accumulated data plus emotional sunk cost, not of features. It takes ~10 years to build. We cannot borrow it.

WHAT FRACTION OF THEIR VALUE IS STRUCTURE VS PURE CHRONOLOGY — stated plainly:
- Apple Journal: ~95% chronology, ~5% structure. Its entire structural vocabulary is: multiple journals (added only in iOS 26, thirty months after launch), a bookmark flag, and two date fields. No tags, no links, no templates, no filters beyond attachment kind. 315,000 ratings at 4.8 stars with essentially zero organisational apparatus is the strongest evidence available that, for reflection use cases, structure is close to worthless.
- Day One: ~80% chronology, ~20% structure. It has tags, collections, filters, templates and a real FTS index — and it buries all of them. The default surfaces are Today, Calendar, On This Day and Streaks. The tagging guide reads like power-user documentation; the On This Day guide reads like product marketing. Fifteen years of iteration converged on time as the primary key.

Both spent their structural budget on ONE thing: making the past come back to you unprompted. Day One does it by anniversary (On This Day, opt-out per journal, with push notifications). Apple does it by proposing entries from your own device activity (Journaling Suggestions). Neither makes you go find anything.

WHY THIS DOESN'T LICENSE US TO DROP STRUCTURE. The asymmetry is failure cost. A journal entry that is never retrieved still delivered its value at write time — the writing was the point. A captured task that is never retrieved is a broken promise, and one broken promise kills trust in the whole system. Day One and Apple are playing a game where retrieval is a bonus; we are playing one where retrieval is the product. Their evidence proves that chronology is sufficient for reflection, not that it is sufficient for action.

THE THREE FINDINGS THAT SHOULD ACTUALLY CHANGE OUR PLAN:

1. NEITHER PRODUCT HAS AN INBOX, AND IT IS DELIBERATE. In both, choosing where a capture goes happens at write time (pick a journal, then write) and is final. There is no unprocessed state, no triage queue, no review step, nothing between raw and archived. Two products with ~433,000 combined US iOS ratings decided a review queue was not worth building. Our confidence gate (0.75/0.45) parks low-confidence items in an Inbox — that is defensible, because our failure cost is different, but it is a real cost these two refused to pay and we should treat the Inbox as a liability to be kept as small as possible, not as a feature. If our gate is mis-tuned and the Inbox grows, we have built the exact thing both market leaders rejected.

2. APPLE JOURNAL CANNOT TRANSCRIBE DUTCH — AND THIS IS OUR OPENING. Apple's own iPhone User Guide states audio transcription is English only, excluding even Singapore/India English variants. Meanwhile the whole app is localised into Dutch as "Dagboek". So on a Flemish user's iPhone, the free preinstalled journal records voice and produces an unsearchable audio blob. Day One is barely better: transcription is iOS-only (Android and Apple Watch cannot transcribe), capped at 10 minutes, off by default, and entirely Apple's on-device stack, so Day One owns none of that pipeline. A Dutch/Flemish voice product with server-side Whisper-class transcription that just works — on any platform, at any length, with the transcript filled in automatically rather than requiring a tap on "Add transcript to entry" — is a genuine gap in a market both giants have left open. This is the single most actionable finding in this report.

3. APPLE'S FREE E2EE RESETS THE PRICING FLOOR AND DEFINES WHERE THE PAID MARKET SURVIVES. Per support.apple.com/en-us/102651, Journal is end-to-end encrypted under both standard and Advanced Data Protection — the same always-E2EE class as passwords and Health, and strictly better than Notes, Photos or Voice Memos. Apple gives away, at zero price and zero configuration, the thing Day One charges $49.99/year for and wraps in a lose-your-key-lose-everything warning. Day One's response is instructive: it did NOT compete on privacy. It raised the annual price from ~$35 (Android Police, Jan 2025) to $49.99 and stacked a $74.99 AI tier on top — and then had to publish this admission: "content from end-to-end encrypted journals is temporarily not end-to-end encrypted while being processed with AI", with the provider described as "our own company, Automattic, or ... a third-party service like OpenAI". That is the E2EE trap in one sentence: the moment you want to understand a capture, you must decrypt it. Our "understand" step (intent, category, project matching, people) makes true E2EE architecturally impossible for us, exactly as it did for Day One. We should decide this consciously and say it plainly in the product rather than discover it later. And the pricing lesson is blunt: privacy and storage are commoditised to zero by Apple; the only defensible paid surface left is the transformation — the thing Apple refuses to do, which is turning a spoken thought into something with a type, an owner and a next step.

FOUR SMALLER TRANSFERABLE MECHANICS:
- Two dates per capture (Apple): entry date vs moment date, sortable by either. Cheap now, expensive to retrofit. Add occurred_at alongside captured_at.
- Per-container behaviour flags (Day One): "Include in Streaks", include-in-On-This-Day, per-journal encryption. Our Projects should carry equivalent switches so one setting governs a class of captures.
- Hashtag-in-message auto-tagging plus a provenance tag (Day One SMS): "#hashtags in your message" become tags, and every texted entry gets a "From Text" tag. Provenance-tagging by capture channel is nearly free and makes debugging our confidence gate far easier — we can measure accuracy per channel.
- Attachments as reorderable, inline-placeable objects (Apple): attachments live in a grid or can be dragged between paragraphs. If a Delta capture ever holds several artefacts (audio, photo, transcript), this is the interaction model to copy.

TWO WARNINGS FROM THEIR MISTAKES:
- Day One cannot fully disable Streaks ("At this time, there is no way to fully disable the Streaks feature"). Guilt mechanics are a retention tactic that becomes a support liability. For a tool people use when stressed and busy, do not build a streak we cannot switch off.
- Day One's search index is a recurring bug source — the 2026.18 notes literally ship "Fixed problems rebuilding the search index", and encrypted journals are invisible to Spotlight. Our spec says "no search index". Given our captures are short and Postgres full-text search is free in Supabase, that is a reasonable call, but note that Day One at fifteen years old still cannot keep a local FTS file healthy. Do not hand-roll one.

METHOD AND LIMITS. The session's WebSearch budget (200/200) was exhausted by a sibling agent before my first search, so discovery ran through DuckDuckGo's HTML endpoint in a shared Playwright browser plus direct WebFetch. Apple's support pages are client-rendered and geo-redirect to nl-nl from this machine, so the Journal documentation was read in Dutch via a real browser and is quoted in Dutch above — this is Apple's own authoritative text, and it incidentally confirms the app's Dutch localisation. I fetched 35+ pages for Day One and 12 for Apple Journal. Explicitly unverified: Day One's Google Play rating and install count (no working listing URL resolved — do not cite a number for this); Apple Journal's version number, build size, release-note history and audio length limits (not exposed on the fetched App Store listing); Day One's AI feature language coverage; which exact fields Day One's FTS index covers (body/title/tags/transcripts/photo OCR are all plausible, none documented); and Apple Journal's offline behaviour. The Android Police price of "$6/month or $35/year" is accurate as of 19 Jan 2025 and is superseded by the $49.99/$74.99 on the current pricing page — I have cited both with dates rather than reconciling them.
```

```text
METHOD AND VERIFICATION NOTES. WebSearch was unavailable for this task (the session had already consumed its 200-search budget on the first call), so everything below was gathered by direct WebFetch against pages I actually retrieved. Roughly 25 pages for Craft and 22 for Superlist. Every figure in the per-app records names the page it came from. Where a page could not be retrieved or a claim could not be corroborated, I marked it "unverified" rather than filling the gap.

PAGES I COULD NOT GET, so the corresponding claims are unverified: (a) Superlist funding round sizes and investors — superlist.com/about is a 404, the blog carries no company history, and no primary funding page was reachable; the only verified corporate facts are from Wikipedia's Wunderlist article. (b) The Google Play listing for Superlist returned truncated content, so Android rating, review count and download numbers are unverified. (c) apps.apple.com HTML pages returned HTTP 429, so I used Apple's public iTunes lookup/search API and the customer-reviews RSS feed instead — these are first-party Apple endpoints and the numbers are solid. (d) feedback.superlist.com (their Frill board) rendered only chrome, so the public feature-request backlog is unverified. (e) I could not reach a conventional independent hands-on review for either product; the closest independent signal I did verify is Product Hunt (Superlist 4.5/5 from 27 reviews, with substantive written criticism), AlternativeTo (Superlist 4.3/5 from 6 ratings, with dated user comments from 2024–2026), and the real App Store customer-review feeds for both. Zapier's best-to-do-list roundup (updated December 2025) covers neither product. thesweetsetup.com and pcmag.com were 404 or unfetchable.

TWO SOURCE CONFLICTS WORTH KNOWING. First, Superlist's help centre is stale: it repeatedly documents a "Pro plan" ("Talk is available as part of our Pro plan"; meeting caps of 30 meetings or 15 hours per month; AI chat at 15 messages/day) while superlist.com/pricing sells only Free, Basic and Super. Mapping Pro onto Basic is my inference and is unverified. Second, Craft's own pricing page and its own docs disagree: the pricing page quotes EUR (Plus €6.40/€8.00, Family for 2–6 members) while the docs quote USD (Plus "$96 annually or $10 monthly", Family "Invite up to 5 members"). Both are cited in the record; do not quote a single Craft price as authoritative.

THE ONE-LINE ANSWER TO THE CENTRAL QUESTION. Superlist does NOT unify a note and a task into one object. It has no Note entity whatsoever. A "note" is a text block; a "task" is a block that additionally carries a checkbox, a due date, a label, an assignee and its own nested detail page which can itself hold blocks. The unification is structural — one block stream containing many block types — not semantic. That is architecturally the opposite of our single actions table with an action_type discriminator. The practical consequence is that Superlist decides what a thing IS at the moment of creation, based on which block type Talk or the user picked, and it can never reclassify. Craft is the same shape: a task is a checkbox block inside a document, with the notable improvement that the identical task appears in space-wide Inbox/Today/Upcoming/All views without duplication.

THE STRATEGIC READ. Both products treat structure as the user's job and AI as an assistant invoked afterwards. Neither has anything resembling our confidence gate, our suggested_* shadow columns, our intent classification, or automatic project and people matching at ingestion. Superlist is the only one of the two with voice, and its own best-practices documentation instructs users to "Talk like you're typing... rather than loosely speaking" and to "Know what you want" before recording — an explicit admission that it handles dictated commands, not unstructured thought. Craft, six years old and 4.84 stars across 6,597 US ratings, has no voice at all. The gap Delta is aiming at is therefore real and, as of today, genuinely unoccupied by either of these two.

THREE UNCOMFORTABLE FACTS FOR OUR PLAN. (1) Superlist has the Wunderlist team, venture funding, and nearly three years in market, and has accumulated only 446 US iOS ratings against Craft's 6,597. Pedigree plus capital plus the exact notes+tasks+AI pitch has not produced traction. The binding constraint in this category is distribution, not model quality, and our plan should reflect that. (2) Craft's 4.84 from 6,597 ratings shows that in this market, sustained design polish outperforms feature convergence — which is bad news for a beginner solo builder competing on craft, and good news only if we compete on an interaction neither of them offers. (3) Both ship in more languages than we will, yet NEITHER has a Dutch UI (Craft: 9 app languages, 11 doc languages, no Dutch; Superlist: EN/DE/ES only). Superlist does transcribe Dutch and Flemish as separate languages, which usefully de-risks the transcription question for our first market while leaving the interface-language niche wide open.

SMALL DETAIL WORTH CORRECTING IN THE BRIEF: the task description says Craft has an "Apple Design Award". craft.do's own marketing page claims Apple Design Awards FINALIST, alongside Mac App of the Year, three Webby Awards and a German Design Award. The ~6,600 US ratings figure in the brief is accurate — the verified number today is 6,597.
```

```text
METHOD AND HONESTY NOTES. The session's WebSearch budget (200 calls) was already exhausted before I started, so discovery was done by driving Bing through a real browser and every substantive fact was then verified by fetching the primary page itself. All Play Store figures were read first-hand from the rendered Play listing in a browser (not a mirror site) on 31 Aug 2026, with the store locale stated per app. Two pages I fetched via a shared Playwright browser were intermittently hijacked by another process navigating the same tab; I verified location.href inside every evaluation and discarded any result whose URL did not match, so no figure here comes from a mispointed page. Anything I could not confirm on a page I actually loaded is marked \"UNVERIFIED\" inline.

THE FLOOR, STATED PLAINLY. From a locked Android phone in Belgium today: 0 taps to create a note or reminder by voice via Gemini (hotword) or 1 power-button hold, in Dutch, free, with the result landing in Google Keep or Google Tasks. That is the number to beat and we cannot beat it — the assistant slot is not available to third parties. From an unlocked phone: 1 tap on Google Keep's Quick capture widget records a voice memo and auto-inserts a transcript alongside the retained audio. That one we CAN match, but only via a third-party Expo widget package, because Expo's own Widgets library is iOS-only.

THE FOUR REAL GAPS IN THE FREE STACK, each verified: (1) No inbox anywhere. Not Keep, not Tasks, not Recorder, not WhatsApp. Nothing in the free stack has an unprocessed state or a review queue, so every capture is filed instantly and irreversibly by an invisible guess. Our confidence gate and Inbox are the feature the entire floor lacks. (2) Create-only routing. Gemini can create a note but per Google's own help page cannot modify or delete one; there is no correction path from voice. (3) Dutch. WhatsApp transcribes English, Portuguese, Spanish and Russian only — no Dutch, confirmed on Meta's own Dutch-language help page. Google Recorder excludes Dutch from real-time transcription and from summaries, offering it only as cloud re-transcription. Samsung's Galaxy AI is the exception and does cover Dutch since Oct 2024. (4) No entity richness. Nothing in the free stack has projects, people, or categories: Keep caps at 50 flat labels, Tasks has one level of subtask and no tags at all.

TIMING. Google Assistant is switched off on Android phones starting 4 September 2026 — four days from this report. Every Android user in our market has their voice-capture habit disrupted that week. That is a launch window and it will not repeat.

WHAT I COULD NOT ESTABLISH AND WOULD TEST ON A REAL DEVICE FIRST: (a) whether Gemini's Keep/Tasks write-actions actually fire on Dutch phrasing on a Belgian phone — the app language list includes Dutch but Google documents nothing about app-action language coverage, and this single test decides how big our wedge is; (b) whether a voice note you send to yourself in a WhatsApp self-chat gets a transcript at all, given the documented rule that \"The sender can't see the transcripts\" — if it does not, WhatsApp's self-chat is even weaker than the language list suggests; (c) whether a Quick Settings tile tap qualifies for Android's while-in-use exemption and can therefore start a microphone foreground service, which decides whether a 2-action locked-phone capture is buildable at all; (d) Google Keep's transcription language coverage, which Google does not document anywhere I could find.

ONE COMPETITIVE SIGNAL WORTH ACTING ON: products explicitly built to fix the WhatsApp self-chat (e.g. luckynote.io, page dated 13 Jul 2026, pitching \"WhatsApp Message Yourself, Made Searchable\") and 2026 blog content arguing the self-chat is \"a pile, not a system\" both indicate the wedge is being noticed by others right now. The habit is validated; the window is not indefinite.
```

```text
METHOD AND LIMITS, STATED UP FRONT. The session's WebSearch budget (200 calls) was already exhausted by earlier agents before I started, so every fact below comes from a URL I fetched directly. I could not install or log into any of the three apps, so all data models are inferred from store descriptions, changelogs, FAQ text and — most usefully — from what reviewers complain is missing. Where I could not verify something I have written "unverified" rather than guessing. Three specific gaps: (a) Coconote's terms of service is an 819 KB PDF I could not extract text from, so its refund and auto-renewal clauses are unverified; (b) the Quizlet–Coconote acquisition is evidenced only by the App Store seller record and the site copyright — TechCrunch's site search returns nothing and quizlet.com/press returns 403, so the date and terms are unverified; (c) Google Play and Reddit block WebFetch, so Play figures were retrieved via curl and parsed from the page HTML, and Reddit sentiment is absent entirely.

=== THE PRICE LADDER, SIDE BY SIDE (all verified from App Store IAP records, 31 Aug 2026) ===
Voice Recorder: weekly $6.99–$7.99 ($363/yr if held) | monthly $16.99 | annual $69.99, deflection SKU $39.99, "Annual Pro" $79.99. 3-day trial baked into the SKU names.
Turbo AI: weekly $9.99 | monthly $19.99, $14.99 | annual $119.99, $89.99, $69.99, $29.99, $19.99 | "50% Off Forever Monthly" $7.49.
Coconote: ten SKUs all named "Coconote Pro" — $9.99, $19.99, $71.99, $99.99, $129.99, $152.99 on iOS; Play range $2.49–$189.99.
Voicenotes, for contrast: free tier of 100 transcription minutes per week plus 30-day history; Pro $9/user/month; Enterprise $24/user/month — all published on a real pricing page at voicenotes.com/pricing.
NOT ONE of the three commodity apps publishes a price on its own website. turbo.ai/pricing is a 404. coconote.app/pricing is a 404. The Voice Recorder has no website. Coconote's homepage says only "Try for $0". This is deliberate: an un-comparable price cannot be shopped.

=== HOW THEY GOT THE RATINGS: FOUR MECHANICS, ALL VERIFIED ===
1. THE NAME IS THE KEYWORD. "Voice Recorder: AI Note Taker" / "Transcribe, Summary & AI Memos". "Coconote - AI Note Taker" / "Lecture Recorder, Minutes". "Turbo AI - Learn Faster" / "AI notes, quizzes, lessons". Against "Voicenotes" — a brand, which nobody types into a search box. The same Suoyi product is called "Voice Memos: AI Note Recorder" on Android because a different string wins there. In a category discovered through a search field, the query IS the product name, and a brand name is a self-inflicted handicap.
2. THE DESCRIPTION IS A COMPETITOR LIST. Coconote's ENTIRE App Store description ends: "Coconote is the more trusted and reliable alternative to Turbo AI, YouLearn, Unstuck, Study Fetch, Quizard, Otter, and Wave AI." Seven competitor brands harvested in one sentence.
3. THE REVIEW PROMPT IS THE PRODUCT'S BUSIEST SCREEN. Two hard pieces of evidence. A Coconote 3★ reviewer, verbatim: "pressured reviews when I haven't had the chance to even really use the product at all yet." And on the Voice Recorder feed, two FIVE-STAR reviews titled "End trial subscription" (Student2260, v1.5.6) and "End of Trial Subscription" (Budget Challenged Student, v1.5.1) — users leaving five-star reviews as a way to ask the developer to cancel their billing, because the app has no support channel. Turbo AI's own App Store page currently features a 5★ review from Mpatel32 whose text is a complaint about the paywall. The rating count and the star average are both partly composed of people who are unhappy. A rating count is a measure of prompt aggression multiplied by install volume, not of quality.
4. PAID AND UNDISCLOSED SOCIAL. TechCrunch (20 Nov 2024, Amanda Silberling) documents the TikTok account @aliistudies posting a video claiming a teacher assigned Coconote's "PDF to Brainrot" tool, drawing 1 million views, and notes it "appears to be undisclosed Coconote sponcon". Turbo AI's Similarweb social traffic comes from YouTube, Instagram and Pinterest. Coconote's from YouTube and Reddit.
Turbo AI adds a fifth: an industrial SEO blog ("Best ChatGPT Alternatives for Studying", "Best StudyFetch Alternatives", "Turbo AI vs. Studley", "Study for AP Calculus with Turbo AI"), producing 1.4M monthly visits.

=== THE CONVERSION TRICK, NAMED ===
It is not a paywall. It is a HOSTAGE PAYWALL, placed after the work is done and the sunk cost is maximal. The archetype is Turbo AI 1★ reviewer Bud907 (v1.4.7): recorded an hour-long lecture, was then asked for $20 to generate the notes, and missed the assignment deadline. Free tiers are sized to guarantee this moment arrives fast: ~1 note set (Turbo AI), ~2 notes (Coconote), 3 recordings/day (Voice Recorder). The weekly SKU exists because at that moment $6.99 feels like nothing. The annual maths ($363/yr) is never shown. Then a named deflection ladder catches the cancel attempt: Turbo AI ships a SKU literally called "50% Off Forever Monthly" at $7.49, plus annual SKUs at $29.99 and $19.99 against a $119.99 list; the Voice Recorder ships "Premium_Yearly_Discount" at $39.99 against $69.99.

=== WHAT THE 1-STAR REVIEWS SAY ABOUT BILLING (verbatim where quoted) ===
Coconote: "I cancelled before the end of the free trial. I was charged, told it was actually a shorter trial than advertised (was in the fine print but said 7 days on the banner 😡. Now 6 months later I was charged $100 out of the blue. I'm not showing any active subscription." And: cancelled an annual plan, told access ran until 12 Dec, "but conveniently it doesn't recognize me and customer service will not respond to my email."
Turbo AI: "Beware of unwanted charges" — $89.99 charged, the only review in the feed with a helpful vote (Laurien86, v1.4.8). "Be aware this Malicious software keeps you in subscribe payments" (BetterAi, v1.5.2). Refund refused despite non-use (Mustafa Bula, v1.5.6). Their terms are unambiguous: "All fees are non-refundable and non-creditable, including for partial periods, except where required by law."
Voice Recorder: "Can't unsubscribe" (Jenbrou). "I didn't authorize the purchase" (Milonsgsmejeheb). Charged after attempting cancellation (ADK68). "Cashgrab for something that apple ALREADY HAS" (Shywolf2) — worth noting, because Apple's own free Voice Memos has 1,074,769 US ratings and is the real floor.
The pattern is identical across all three and it is structural, not accidental: short or misrepresented trials, a cancel flow that fails, no support channel, and no refund.

=== THE STRATEGIC ANSWER, BLUNTLY ===
An anonymous shovelware app outranking Voicenotes on installed base does not mean the market prefers bad products. It means INSTALLED BASE IN THIS CATEGORY IS NOT A PRODUCT-QUALITY SIGNAL AT ALL. It is a distribution artifact of one search box, and it is purchasable with keyword-stuffed names, competitor-name harvesting, undisclosed TikTok sponcon, and a review prompt fired at the moment of maximum confusion. Voicenotes lost that race because it was named after itself and gave people 100 free minutes a week instead of forcing a paywall in minute three. It lost the race on the metric it was never running. Six specific consequences for Delta:

1. YOU CANNOT WIN THE APP STORE SEARCH BOX. Do not plan as though you can. You have no ASO budget, no sponcon budget, a brand name nobody searches, and Turbo AI is adding 20,000 users a day. Every user you get will come from somewhere other than a store search. Decide what that somewhere is BEFORE you build more features — that is the actual open question in this project, not brain-versus-assistant.

2. THE PRICE FLOOR IS NOT ZERO AND IT IS NOT LOW. The default SKU in this tier is $6.99–$9.99 PER WEEK. Delta at €10/month undercuts the commodity tier's headline price by roughly 70%. You are not being undercut on price; you are being undercut on the LEGIBILITY of price. Publish a real pricing page with the annual maths shown. In a segment where all three competitors hide their price and two return 404 on /pricing, publishing one is a positioning statement that costs you a weekend.

3. YOUR DIFFERENTIATOR IS INVISIBLE AT INSTALL TIME AND THAT IS YOUR CENTRAL PROBLEM. Pre-purchase, the only legible differences in this category are: does it record, does it transcribe, does it summarise. All three of those are now commodity inputs bought from the same suppliers — the Suoyi app openly lists Whisper, Deepgram Nova-3, Gemini 3.1 Flash, GPT-5 and DeepSeek V4.0, which is the same shelf you buy from. Intent detection, a confidence gate, project matching and people links are only legible after weeks of accumulated captures. That means Delta structurally CANNOT be sold with a session-one paywall — which is precisely the only monetisation model proven to work here. Do not paper over this tension. Resolve it deliberately: monetise session 30, not session 1, which means a genuinely usable free tier (Voicenotes' 100 min/week is the right shape) and revenue that arrives late. Budget for that runway or change the product.

4. DO NOT SELL TO STUDENTS. The commodity tier owns the student, exam-cram, disposable-artifact use case, and it owns it with a cost structure you cannot match. Delta's entities — projects with summaries, people with roles, actions with status — only have value to someone with ongoing work that spans weeks. A student churns at $6.99/week; a Flemish consultant pays €10/month without thinking about it. The $0–7/week floor only constrains you if you sell to the same buyer. Segment out of it.

5. THE EU/DUTCH PRIVACY WEDGE IS THE SHARPEST THING AVAILABLE TO YOU AND IT IS FREE. The Voice Recorder lists Dutch FIRST in its language array, is operated from Hangzhou, routes user speech to Deepgram, OpenAI, Google and DeepSeek, and its privacy policy is a copy-pasted dating-app document that discusses "match durations" and "your conversation partner reports your abusive behavior" and never once mentions audio. Coconote's policy names ZERO sub-processors and is silent on whether recordings train models. Turbo AI's says fees are non-refundable and is governed from Frisco, Texas. A Belgian developer offering EU hosting, a named sub-processor list, an explicit "your audio never trains a model", and a real DPA is making a claim none of the three can make — and, more importantly, none of them will ever bother to make, because their buyer is a panicking student at 11pm, not a professional with a compliance reflex. This is not a feature. It is the segmentation boundary.

6. THE REAL THREAT IS TURBO AI, NOT THE ANONYMOUS ONE. Suoyi is a rent-extractor with a dead 2022 website, a three-month-stale build, and eleven other apps; it will never build structure because structure does not convert in week one. Turbo AI is a profitable 15-person team on eight-figure ARR raised on $750K, shipping fast, already adding folders and Learn Mode, already running pricing A/B tests. If anyone in this tier moves upmarket into persistent structure, it is them. Watch their changelog, not the commodity apps'.

FINAL WARNING ON DELTA'S OWN DESIGN. The confidence gate and Inbox add a decision at exactly the moment these apps optimise to remove one: capture-to-artifact. If a new user's first session ends with "10 items parked in your Inbox for review", Delta loses to an app that ends with a finished summary — and Turbo AI has already run this experiment for you: their v1.5.2 update replaced automatic lecture notes with an interactive chat flow and generated a wall of 1★ reviews demanding the old behaviour back ("Bring Back Automatic Lecture Notes", "They changed everything..it's useless now"). The Inbox must be near-invisible in week one and indispensable in month two. That single design problem decides whether the structure thesis survives contact with real users, and it is worth more of your time than any feature currently on the board.

CROSS-CHECK ON THE PREMISE. Verified 31 Aug 2026 via the iTunes lookup API: Voice Recorder: AI Note Taker has 7,517 US ratings (4.66); Voicenotes AI Notes & Meetings has 6,719 (4.77). Both launched within two months of each other in 2024 (16 Jun and 16 Apr respectively). The premise holds. But note Apple's own free Voice Memos sits at 1,074,769 US ratings — the true floor in this category is a free first-party app that ships on every iPhone, and one of the Suoyi 1★ reviewers has already noticed ("Cashgrab for something that apple ALREADY HAS"). Any pitch Delta makes has to survive that sentence, and "it records and summarises" does not.

Sources for the cross-cutting claims above: https://itunes.apple.com/lookup?id=6502794561&country=us, https://itunes.apple.com/lookup?id=6479320349&country=us, https://itunes.apple.com/lookup?id=6503227362&country=us, https://itunes.apple.com/search?term=voicenotes&country=us&entity=software&limit=5, https://itunes.apple.com/us/rss/customerreviews/id=6502794561/sortBy=mostRecent/json, https://itunes.apple.com/us/rss/customerreviews/id=6479320349/sortBy=mostRecent/json, https://itunes.apple.com/us/rss/customerreviews/id=6503227362/sortBy=mostRecent/json, https://apps.apple.com/us/app/turbo-ai-learn-faster/id6502794561, https://apps.apple.com/us/app/coconote-ai-note-taker/id6479320349, https://apps.apple.com/us/app/voice-recorder-ai-note-taker/id6503227362, https://www.turbo.ai/terms-of-service, https://www.turbo.ai/blog, https://techcrunch.com/2025/10/23/20-year-old-dropouts-built-ai-notetaker-turbo-ai-to-5-million-users/, https://techcrunch.com/2024/11/20/pdf-to-brainrot-study-tools-are-a-strange-iteration-on-a-tiktok-trend/, https://voicenotes.com/pricing, https://magictool.net/voicememos/protocol/privacy.html, https://www.similarweb.com/website/turbo.ai/, https://www.similarweb.com/website/coconote.app/, https://www.similarweb.com/website/voicenotes.com/, https://www.similarweb.com/website/magictool.net/
```
